## 1.3. Eligibility to the centralised procedure

The applicant Shionogi B.V. submitted on 27 June 2025 an application for marketing authorisation to the European Medicines Agency (EMA) for Zokovea (ensitrelvir), through the centralised procedure falling within the Article 3(1) and point 3 of Annex of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004. The eligibility to the centralised procedure was agreed upon by the EMA/CHMP on 27 January 2022.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The applicant applied for the following indication:

Zokovea is indicated for the treatment of coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) in adults and adolescents aged 12 years and older, who do not require hospitalisation for severe disease and postexposure prophylaxis of COVID-19 in adults and adolescents aged 12 years and older.

## 1.4. Legal basis and dossier content

## The legal basis for this application refers to:

Article 8.3 of Directive 2001/83/EC - complete and independent application

The application submitted is composed of administrative information, complete quality data, and nonclinical and clinical data based on applicant's own tests and studies and bibliographic literature substituting/supporting certain tests or studies.

## 1.5. Information on paediatrics

Pursuant to Article 7 of Regulation (EC) No 1901/2006, the application included EMA decisions P/0242/2024 on the agreement of a paediatric investigation plan (PIP) for treatment of coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) and EMEA-003192-PIP03-23 on the agreement of a PIP for post-exposure prophylaxis of coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19).

At the time of submission of the application, the PIPs P/0242/2024 and EMEA-003192-PIP03-23 had not yet been completed as some measures had been deferred.

The PDCO issued on 18 October 2024 a positive partial compliance check for PIP P/0242/2024 in relation to Study 3 and on 18 June 2025 a positive partial compliance check for PIP EMEA-003192PIP03-23 in relation Study 5.

| Agreed studies/measures (study identifier)   | Description                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
|----------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Study 3 (S-217622-TF-200-L) PIP P/0242/2024  | Toxicity and toxicokinetic study in SD rats from postnatal days 4 to 20, to support the development of ensitrelvir for use in humans from birth to less than 2 years of age                                                                    |
| Study 5 (SCORPIO-PEP) EMEA-003192-PIP03-23   | Randomized, double-blind, parallel-group, placebo-controlled comparative study in adolescents from 12 years to less than 18 years of age (and adults) who are uninfected household contacts of symptomatic SARS-CoV-2- infected index patients |

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 1.6. Information on orphan market exclusivity

## 1.6.1. Similarity with authorised orphan medicinal products

Pursuant to Article 8 of Regulation (EC) No 141/2000 and Article 3 of Commission Regulation (EC) No 847/2000, the applicant did not submit a critical report addressing the possible similarity with authorised orphan medicinal products from the start of the procedure because there is no authorised orphan medicinal product for a condition related to the proposed indication.

## 1.7. Applicant's request(s) for consideration

## 1.7.1. New active substance status

The applicant requested the active substance ensitrelvir contained in the above medicinal product to be considered as a new active substance, as the applicant claims that it is not a constituent of a medicinal product previously authorised within the European Union.

## 1.7.1.1. CHMP recommendation on new active substance status

Based on the review of available data on the active substance, the CHMP considers that ensitrelvir is to be qualified as a new active substance in itself, as it is not a constituent of a medicinal product previously authorised within the European Union.

## 1.9.3. Opinion

Based on the CHMP review of data on quality, safety and efficacy, the CHMP considers by consensus that the benefit-risk balance of Zokovea is favourable in the following indication:

Zokovea is indicated for the post-exposure prophylaxis of COVID-19 in adults and adolescents aged 12 years and older. Zokovea should be used in accordance with official recommendations.

The CHMP, therefore, recommends the granting of the marketing authorisation subject to the conditions described in the following sections.

## 1.9.8. Proposed list of recommendations

Table 2: Proposed list of recommendations

## Description of recommendation(s)

To conduct annual resistance analyses reporting on the frequency of amino acid substitutions that have been identified in the studies to date, and that have not been identified, but are within 5Å of the binding site, in SARS-CoV-2 sequences from Europe and globally to determine levels of circulating ensitrelvir resistance.

In the event of a pandemic due to a novel (non-SARS-CoV-2) pneumonic coronavirus deemed susceptible to ensitrelvir, to conduct a treatment study to investigate the efficacy of ensitrelvir in that setting. The design, objectives and key features of this study are intended to be further discussed at a subsequent Scientific Advice.

## 2. Introduction

## 2.1. Therapeutic context

Coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) is a highly contagious infectious disease caused by severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 (SARS-CoV-2). Symptoms of COVID-19 result from an inflammatory or immune response to viral infection and include fever, cough, nasal congestion, rhinorrhoea, sore throat, muscle aches, diarrhoea, and taste and smell disorders. Several of these symptoms are similar to symptoms of influenza, with additional manifestations frequently reported.

The incubation period of COVID-19 ranges from 1 to 14 days, with symptom onset typically occurring approximately 5 days after exposure to earlier variants of SARS-CoV-2 and approximately 3 days after exposure to the Omicron variant (CDC, 2021). The majority of infections are mild to moderate in severity, with recovery generally observed within 1 week after onset of symptoms (NIH, 2024). SARSCoV-2 may be highly transmissible from 2 days before symptom onset until 7 to 10 days after onset (MHLW, 2022).

Some patients may experience severe disease and require hospitalization and oxygen treatment. The known risk factors for severe COVID-19 include age (65 years or older), hypertension, obesity, and smoking (CDC, 2023a), and the risk of mortality increases with increasing age. A certain proportion of infected individuals may remain asymptomatic. Various symptoms of COVID-19 may persist or recur for prolonged periods in some patients even after recovery, a condition typically referred to as 'long COVID'.

Given that the early phase of COVID-19 is associated with viral replication and the resulting inflammatory response, initiation of antiviral treatment at an early stage to quickly disrupt viral replication and alleviate the inflammatory response to infection is expected to prevent progression to severe disease.

Vaccination against SARS-CoV-2 is the major public health intervention to prevent severe disease. While individuals who are up to date with COVID-19 vaccination are generally protected against severe disease, the risk of symptomatic infection remains. Moreover, the uptake of COVID-19 vaccines has decreased substantially compared to that observed early in the pandemic, leaving many high-risk individuals vulnerable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

There are currently limited therapeutic options for the treatment of COVID-19. Nirmatrelvir/ritonavir (targets the main protease of SARS-CoV-2 similar to ensitrelvir) and remdesivir (interferes with replication of the viral RNA) are indicated for patients at increased risk of progressing to severe COVID-19. Moreover, remdesivir is indicated for patients with pneumonia requiring supplemental oxygen.

Other therapies, designed to neutralize the virus by binding to its spike protein, face reduced efficacy as the virus continues to evolve.

Thus, no therapy is currently approved with the primary objective of reducing symptom burden, shortening time to recovery, and reducing the risk of long-term sequelae. There are no approved oral antivirals for the post-exposure prophylaxis of COVID-19.

## 2.2. Aspects of development

A total of ten completed clinical studies, including seven Phase 1 studies, one Phase 2/3 study, and two Phase 3 studies, were conducted as part of the clinical development programme of ensitrelvir.

The primary efficacy and safety data supporting the use ensitrelvir in the treatment of mild/moderate COVID-19 were based on the completed pivotal studies SCORPIO-SR (2108T1221, Phase 3 part) and the Phase 3 SCORPIO-HR (ACTIV-2d/A5407).

The primary efficacy and safety of ensitrelvir as post-exposure prophylaxis was based on data from the pivotal SCORPIO-PEP Phase 3 study (2206T1331).

## 2.3. Description of the product

Zokovea is presented as a tablet. Each tablet contains ensitrelvir fumaric acid equivalent to 125 mg of ensitrelvir.

Ensitrelvir selectively inhibits SARS-CoV-2 3C-like (3CL) protease, an essential enzyme for the processing of the polyprotein encoded by the SARS CoV-2 gene, which thereby inhibits SARS-CoV-2 replication. The 50% inhibitory concentration (IC50) of ensitrelvir for SARS-CoV-2 3CL protease activity was 0.0132 μmol/L.

The applicant proposed the following indications:

- the treatment of coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) in adults and adolescents aged 12 years and older, who do not require hospitalisation for severe disease (withdrawn in the course of the procedure - on 9 July 2026)
- post-exposure prophylaxis of COVID-19 in adults and adolescents aged 12 years and older.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The recommended dose for patients 12 years and older is 375 mg of ensitrelvir orally once daily on Day 1 and 125 mg once daily from Days 2 to 5 for a total of 5 days.

## 3. Quality aspects

## 3.1. Introduction

The finished product is presented as tablets containing 125 mg of ensitrelvir active substance (as fumaric acid cocrystal).

Other ingredients are: mannitol (E421), croscarmellose sodium (E468), hydroxypropyl cellulose (E463), silica colloidal anhydrous (E551), microcrystalline cellulose (E460) and magnesium stearate (E470b).

The product is available in in PVC/PCTFE with aluminium foil blisters, as described in section 6.5 of the SmPC.

## 3.2. Active substance

## 3.2.1. General information

The chemical name of ensitrelvir fumaric acid is (E)-6-((6-chloro-2-methyl-2H-indazol-5-yl)imino)-3((1-methyl-1H-1,2,4-triazol-3-yl)methyl)-1-(2,4,5-trifluorobenzyl)-1,3,5-triazinane-2,4-dione fumaric acid corresponding to the molecular formula C22H17ClF3N9O2. It has a relative molecular mass of 647.96 and the following structure:

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Figure 1: Active substance structure

<!-- image -->

The chemical structure of ensitrelvir fumaric acid was elucidated by a combination of appropriate analytical techniques.

The solid-state properties of the active substance were measured by thermal analysis, single crystal Xray crystallography and X-ray powder diffraction (XRPD). The crystalline form of ensitrelvir fumaric acid does not change during storage.

The active substance is a white non-hygroscopic powder. It is practically insoluble in aqueous media across the physiological pH range.

Ensitrelvir fumaric acid has a non-chiral molecular structure.

## 3.2.2. Manufacture, characterisation, and process controls

Full information on the active substance manufacture is submitted as part of the application.

The active substance is manufactured at multiple sites. Satisfactory evidence of GMP compliance has been presented for each manufacturing site.

Ensitrelvir fumaric acid is synthesised in multiple steps using well-defined starting materials with acceptable specifications.

1Adequate in-process controls are applied during the synthesis. The specifications and control methods for intermediate products, starting materials and reagents have been presented.

The characterisation of the active substance and its impurities are in accordance with the EU guideline on the chemistry of active substances. Potential and actual impurities were well discussed with regards to their origin and characterised. An evaluation of potential mutagenic impurities was performed in accordance with ICH M7 and the proposed control strategy is considered acceptable.

The commercial manufacturing process for the active substance was developed in parallel with the clinical development program. Changes introduced have been presented in sufficient detail and have been justified. Except for the first small-scale batch manufactured according to process A, all subsequent batches were produced by process B which introduced further process optimisation to improve yield and robustness. The quality of the active substance used in the various phases of the development is thus comparable with that produced by the proposed commercial process.

The manufacturing process has been developed using a combination of conventional univariate studies and elements of QbD such as risk assessment based on Failure Mode and Effect Analysis (FMEA). Proven acceptable ranges (PARs) have been defined for several steps in the manufacturing process of the active substance. The available development data, the proposed control strategy and batch analysis data fully support the proposed PARs, whereas normal operating ranges (NORs) are described in the manufacturing process description for routine operation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Ensitrelvir fumaric acid is stored in double low-density polyethylene (LDPE) bags tightly closed with either cable, zip or twist ties, and placed in a suitable secondary container (i.e., a fiber or high-density polyethylene container). The LDPE bags comply with Ph. Eur. 3.1.3. Polyolefins and Regulation (EU) No. 10/2011 on plastic materials and articles intended to come into contact with food, as amended. The container closure system used for the active substance is considered suitable for its intended used and the information presented is considered acceptable.

## 3.2.3. Specification

The active substance specification includes tests for description (visual), identification (IR), fumaric acid content (HPLC), related substances (HPLC), residual solvents (GC), water, (Ph. Eur.), sulfated ash (Ph. Eur.), assay (HPLC).

3The active substance specification is appropriately justified and based on the critical quality attributes (CQAs). Acceptance limits are generally in line with ICH Q3A, ICH Q3C, ICH Q3D, ICH M7 guidelines, Ph. Eur., batch release and stability data.

The analytical procedures used have been adequately described and (non-compendial methods) appropriately validated in accordance with the ICH guidelines. Satisfactory information regarding the reference standards used has been presented.

Batch analysis data are provided and cover the different manufacturers involved the synthetic steps. The results are within the specifications and consistent from batch to batch.

## 3.2.4. Stability

The stability data presented for the active substance comprises stability results from four stability conducted under long term (25 °C/60 %RH) and accelerated conditions (40 °C/75 %RH), stress testing (solid state in bottle at 60 °C and on Petri dish at 25 °C/60 %RH and 40 °C/75 %RH for 3 months), photostability testing in accordance with ICH Q1B, and forced degradation (in solution under acidic, basic, light, and oxidative conditions) studies. Since the quality of the active substance batches presented display a high level of consistency during development the results from all stability studies presented are regarded as representative for later commercial batches of active substance.

The stability results indicate that the active substance manufactured by the proposed suppliers is sufficiently stable. The stability results justify the proposed retest period of 48 months when stored below 30°C in the proposed container.

The applicant commits to continue the ongoing stability studies of active substance and to place at least one batch per year, unless none is produced during that year, on stability studies. Any confirmed out-of-specification result, or significant negative trend, should be reported to the Rapporteur and EMA.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3.3. Finished medicinal product

## 3.3.1. Description of the product and pharmaceutical development

The finished product is an immediate release tablets for oral administration containing ensitrelvir fumaric acid active substance equivalent to 125 mg of ensitrelvir as free base. 4

The pharmaceutical development of the finished product contains QbD elements. A quality target product profile (QTPP) was established with the aim to develop an immediate release oral dosage form which is suitable for patient acceptance and compliance and ensures patient safety and product efficacy throughout shelf life. The critical quality attributes identified were description, assay, identification, uniformity of dosage units, dissolution, related substances and microbiological quality.

5The physicochemical properties of the active substance that may influence of the finished product tablet performance are adequately discussed. The crystal form does not change during manufacture and storage of the finished product.

All excipients are well known pharmaceutical ingredients and their quality is compliant with Ph. Eur. standards. There are no novel excipients used in the finished product formulation.

The manufacturing process is based on a standard dry granulation process.

The manufacturing development has been evaluated through the use of risk assessment in line with ICH Q9 principles to identify the critical process parameters to define critical process steps and process parameters that may have an influence on the finished product quality attributes. The risk identification was based on the prior knowledge of products with similar formulations and manufacturing processes as well as on the experience from formulation development, process design and scale-up studies. The critical process parameters have been adequately identified.

Dissolution is the physicochemical property that affects in vivo performance of the finished product. The dissolution method was developed based on the solubility characteristics of the active substance and release profiles obtained for an ensitrelvir co-crystal tablet and a free-form tablet.

The primary packaging is a polyvinyl chloride/polychlorotrifluoroethylene (PVC/PCTFE) with aluminium foil blister. The material complies with Ph. Eur. and EC requirements. The choice of the container closure system has been validated by stability data and is adequate for the intended use of the product.

## 3.3.2. Manufacture of the product and process controls

The finished product is manufactured by multiple sites. Satisfactory evidence of GMP compliance has been provided for all sites involved in the manufacturing, testing and batch release of the finished product.

The manufacturing process consists of multiple steps based on a dry blend manufacturing process. The process is considered to be standard manufacturing for immediate release tablets.

2The manufacturing process and its control, as well as critical equipment have been described in sufficient details. Proven acceptable ranges have been defined for several steps in the manufacturing process. The available development data, the proposed control strategy and batch analysis data from commercial scale batches fully support the proposed PARs.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The maximum holding times for intermediate and bulk products have been justified with stability data. The manufacturing process at two proposed manufacturing sites has been validated on 3 consecutive batches at the proposed commercial batch size. It has been demonstrated that the manufacturing process is capable of producing the finished product of intended quality in a reproducible manner. The in-process controls are adequate for this type of manufacturing process and pharmaceutical form.

The validation protocol submitted by the applicant for process validation at another manufacturing site is considered acceptable. The process will be validated prior to commercialisation by the manufacture of three production batches at the proposed commercial batch size.

## 3.3.3. Product specification

The finished product release and shelf-life specification include appropriate tests for this type of dosage form: description (visual), identification (HPLC/PDA), degradation products (HPLC), uniformity of dosage units (Ph. Eur.), dissolution (Ph. Eur.), assay (HPLC) and microbiological examination (Ph. Eur.).

6The finished product specification is justified with reference to Ph. Eur. requirements, ICH guidelines (ICHQ6B, ICHQ3B, ICHQ3D) and supported with testing results from clinical, process validation, and stability batches of 125 mg tablets manufactured at commercial, pilot or laboratory-scale.

The potential presence of elemental impurities in the finished product has been assessed following a risk-based approach in line with the ICH Q3D Guideline for Elemental Impurities. Batch analysis data demonstrate that each relevant elemental impurity was not detected above 30% of the respective PDE. Based on the risk assessment and the presented batch data it can be concluded that it is not necessary to include any elemental impurity controls. The information on the control of elemental impurities is satisfactory.

The initial risk assessment concerning the potential presence of nitrosamine impurities in the finished product was not complete and only brief summaries were presented instead. As a consequence, two major objections (MOs) were raised by CHMP requesting the applicant to submit the entire nitrosamine risk assessment performed for both the active substance (MO1) and the finished product (MO2). The applicant appropriately responded to both MOs by submitting the updated risk assessments considering all suspected and actual root causes in line with the 'Questions and answers for marketing authorisation holders/applicants on the CHMP Opinion for the Article 5(3) of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004 referral on nitrosamine impurities in human medicinal products' (EMA/409815/2020) and the 'Assessment report- Procedure under Article 5(3) of Regulation EC (No) 726/2004- Nitrosamine impurities in human medicinal products' (EMA/369136/2020).

In particular, the presence of vulnerable amines and nitrosating agents in the synthesis of the active substance was addressed. For the finished product, the updated risk assessment elaborated on the risk of nitrosating agents from the excipients in the tablet formulation. Based on the information provided, it is accepted that there is no risk of nitrosamine impurities in the active substance or the related finished product. Therefore, no specific control measures are deemed necessary.

The analytical methods used have been adequately described and appropriately validated in accordance with the ICH guidelines. Satisfactory information regarding the reference standards used for assay and fumaric acid testing has been presented.

Batch analysis results have been provided for a large number of batches covering different batches sizes and purposes (clinical, stability, process validation) confirming the consistency of the manufacturing process and its ability to manufacture to the intended product specification.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3.3.4. Stability of the product

Stability data were presented on multiple production scale batches in several ongoing ICH stability studies and manufactured at the proposed commercial manufacturing sites.

In addition, one batch was exposed to light as defined in the ICH Guideline on Photostability Testing of New Drug Substances and Products. Results of the light stressed sample show no significant differences with the dark controls and thus the finished product is not sensitive to light.

A bulk hold stability study was performed which justifies the declared holding time in the manufacturing description.

Based on available stability data, the proposed shelf-life of 3 years and not requiring any special storage condition as stated in the SmPC (section 6.3) are acceptable.

In accordance with EU GMP guidelines, any confirmed out-of-specification result, or significant negative trend with respect to the ongoing stability programs, should be reported to the Rapporteur and EMA.

## Adventitious agents

No excipients derived from animal or human origin have been used.

## 3.4. Discussion and conclusions on chemical, pharmaceutical and biological aspects

Information on development, manufacture and control of the active substance and finished product has been presented in a satisfactory manner. The applicant has applied QbD principles in the development of the active substance and/or finished product and their manufacturing process. However, no design spaces were claimed for the manufacturing process of the active substance, nor for the finished product. The results of tests performed indicate consistency and uniformity of important product quality characteristics, and these in turn lead to the conclusion that the product should have a satisfactory and uniform performance in clinical use.

During the procedure 2 MOs were raised by the CHMP related to the incomplete nitrosamine impurities risk assessment in the active substance (MO1) and the finished product (MO2). Both MOs were satisfactorily resolved by submitting the updated risk assessments with consideration of all suspected and actual root causes in line with the EMA requirements, particularly addressing the risk of vulnerable amines and nitrosating agents in the synthesis of the active substance, and addressing risk of nitrosating agents from the excipients in the finished product tablet formulation.

The quality of this product is considered to be acceptable when used in accordance with the conditions defined in the SmPC. Physicochemical and biological aspects relevant to the uniform clinical performance of the product have been investigated and are controlled in a satisfactory way.

## 3.5. Recommendation(s) for future quality development

Not applicable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4. Non-clinical aspects

## 4.1. Introduction

The applicant has submitted a non-clinical study package in accordance with the ICH-M3 guideline on 'Non-clinical safety studies for the conduct of human clinical trials for pharmaceuticals'. Pharmacology studies included in vitro and in vivo evaluation of pharmacodynamic properties of ensitrelvir. Toxicology studies included repeat-dose administration with a duration of up to 4 weeks in rats and cynomolgus monkeys, covering the intended clinical treatment duration of 5 days.

An Environmental Risk Assessment (ERA) in accordance with the updated 2024 guideline was also submitted.

All toxicology pivotal studies and Safety Pharmacology studies were conducted in compliance with GLP.

## 4.2. Analytical methods

The applicant has submitted method validations reports as part of the MAA. LC-MS/MS assays were validated for the quantitation of ensitrelvir in plasma over a concentration range of 0.1-200000 (µg/mL). The bioanalytical methods for determination of ensitrelvir in plasma were developed and successful validated. Measurement of radioactivity in the in vitro studies was measured with a liquid scintillation counter (LSC) and in the distribution study by means of quantitative whole-body autoradiography (QWBA) using an imaging plate analyser. High performance liquid chromatography with radio-detector (radio-HPLC) was used in the metabolism study. In addition, a report (S-217622QB-003-N) described the preparation of [ 14 C]-labelled ensitrelvir fumaric acid for ADME studies.

## 4.3. Pharmacology

## 4.3.1. Pharmacodynamics

## 4.3.1.1. Primary pharmacodynamics

The in vivo anti-viral effects of ensitrelvir fumaric acid were investigated in mouse models of SARSCoV-2 infection. The Applicant has submitted 5 primary pharmacodynamic studies. Ensitrelvir fumaric acid was orally administered to mice inoculated intranasally with either the SARS-CoV-2 gamma variant (Pango lineage P.1: hCoV-19/Japan/TY7-501/2021 strain) or the MA-P10 strain (mouseadapted hCoV-19/Japan/TY/WK-521/2020). All studies were performed in female BALB/c mice.

In study S-217622-EB-018-N pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic analysis of ensitrelvir on SARSCoV-2 replication in mice was conducted. Ensitrelvir was administered orally once daily, twice daily (every 12 hours) or 4 time daily (every 6 hours) for a total dosing period of one day at doses of 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, and 64 mg/kg. The first administration of ensitrelvir was performed immediately after administration of the virus (Pango lineage P.1: hCoV-19/Japan/TY7-501/2021 strain) at 1.00 × 10 4 TCID50 virus suspension under anaesthesia. Measurements of viral titres were performed 24h after the first dose and showed a decrease of viral titres in lung at all tested doses and treatment modalities, indicative of inhibition of viral replication. The decrease of viral titres was dose dependent and was observed for all treatment modalities.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In study S-217622-EB-081 the first administration of ensitrelvir was given 24 hours after virus infection at doses 8, 16, 32, and 64 mg/kg with dose modalities bid (every 12 hours) for 2 days; 8, 16, 32, and 64 mg/kg 3 times daily (tid) (every 8 hours) for 2 days; 16, 32, and 64 mg/kg qd for 2 days; and 32 and 64 mg/kg qd for 1 day. A dose-dependent reduction of viral titres in lungs was observed, with the most pronounced reduction, compared to control, when ensitrelvir fumaric acid was administered three time daily, followed by twice daily and once daily administration.

The therapeutic effect of ensitrelvir after 24h in mice infected with SARS-CoV-2 was evaluated in study S-217622 EB-099-N. The viral strain used in this study differs from the one used in the two previous studies. Here SARS-CoV-2 MA-P10 strain (mouse-adapted hCoV-19/Japan/TY/WK-521/2020) was inoculated intra-nasally in female BALB/c mice at titre of 1.00 × 10 3 TCID50/mouse. Survival and body wight were evaluated up to 14 days post infection. With respect to survival and body weight significant effects were noted at of 8, 16, and 32 mg/kg of ensitrelvir. At the doses 8, 16, and 32 mg/kg the survival rate was 100%. For 4 mg/kg and vehicle treatment the corresponding figures were 91.7% and 37.5%, respectively. For body weight a rate of reduction up to 5 days after infection was suppressed in a dose-dependent manner.

The prophylactic effects with respect to survival and body weight changes of ensitrelvir against lethal infection with SARS-CoV-2 strain (mouse adapted hCoV-19/Japan/TY/WK-521/2020) in mice, intranasally inoculated with 3.00 × 10 2 TCID50, was evaluated in study S-217622-EB-171-N. Ensitrelvir was administered as a single subcutaneously, 24 hours prior to virus inoculation, at doses of 0 (vehicle), 32, 64, 96 and 128 mg/kg. Survival and body weight were monitored for 14 days after virus infection and for the highest dose of 128 mg/kg survival was 100% at 14 days, followed by 80% (96 mg/kg), 60% (64 mg/kg), 6.7% (32 mg/kg), exhibiting dose-dependency. For the vehicle treatment all animals were dead at day eight. The animals dosed at 64, 96 and 128 mg/kg of ensitrelvir did also exhibit a significant suppression of body weight loss. The minimal plasma concentration to mediate a prophylactic effect was 2990 ng/mL.

The inhibitory prophylactic effect was further evaluated in study S-217622-EB-189-N in lethally infected mice with the mouse adapted hCoV-19/Japan/TY/WK-521/2020 (SARS-CoV-2 MA-P10) strain in mice. Lung virus titres measured 1, 2, 4 and 9 days after infection showed that virus titres peaked at day 2 and then decreased in all dose groups. At day nine the survival rate of animals was lowest in the vehicle treated group, were only 3/10 animals survived compared to that of animals dosed at 64 mg/kg (8/10) and 128 mg/kg (9/10). The virus titres (log10 TCID50/mL) at this time-point however did not differ between animals treated with vehicle, 64 mg/kg and 128 mg/kg.

## 4.3.1.2. Secondary pharmacodynamics

The Applicant has submitted 4 studies evaluating the secondary pharmacodynamics of ensitrelvir. In order to confirm selectivity in vitro inhibition of 108 molecular targets (49 receptors, 19 ion channels, 6 transporters, 31 enzymes, and 3 kinases) were evaluated in a non-GLP compliant study at a concentration of 100 µmol/L. Out of a total of 108 assayed molecular targets, 9 exhibited more than 50 % inhibition in either specific radioligand binding or enzymatic activity. These were PDE4A1A, PDE4B1, PDE4C1, PDE4D2, PDE7A, PDE7B, cholecystokinin (CCK) 2 receptor, nicotinic acetylcholine (ACh) receptor subunit α1, and adenosine transporter.

A follow-up dose response test was performed on 19 targets and included including 10 peptidases (angiotensin converting enzyme, caspase 2, chymotrypsin, cathepsin B, cathepsin D, cathepsin G, cathepsin L, neutrophil elastase 2, human immunodeficiency virus (HIV)-1 protease and thrombin), as well as the 9 positive targets in the initial screening.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

For the tested molecular targets with more than 50% inhibition or stimulation the IC50 values for the enzyme activities of PDE4A1A, PDE4B1, PDE4C1, and PDE4D2 was 63.2, 69.1, 75.7, and 73.4 μmol/L, respectively. There were no effects up to a concentration of 100 μmol/L (53.2 μg/mL) on other receptors, ion channels, transporters or enzymes. The IC50 value for adenosine uptake by the adenosine transporter was 1.43 μmol/L.

The unbound Cmax concentration expected with 375/125 mg of ensitrelvir is 1.2 μmol/L. Thus, the IC50 values were well above (53 to 63 times) the unbound Cmax concentration except for the adenosine transporter which had IC50 1.43 µmol/L, corresponding to a margin of 1,2x of unbound Cmax in the clinical setting. Nevertheless, the potential inhibition of PDE4 is further discussed in the toxicology section of this AR.

As presented and assessed in the Toxicology section 4.4.8, additional off-target effect of ensitrelvir fumaric acid was also noted on plasma LCAT (Lecithin:Cholesterol Acyltransferase) activity, evaluated in monkey plasma. The data showed that ensitrelvir had an inhibitory effect on LCAT exhibiting inhibition rates ranging from 2.08% to 82.86%. However, no inhibition was noted for cholesterol efflux activity by HDL.

CC50 values for 9 cell lines and 3 primary cultured human cells from different human issues were estimated to exceed 72 μg/mL (135 μmol/L). No mitochondrial toxicity in HepG2 cells was observed up to 300 μg/mL (564 μmol/L). Since there were about 140-fold or more difference between EC50 values (0.026 to 0.99 μmol/L) for each SARS-CoV-2 strain and CC50 values of ensitrelvir fumaric acid, it was judged that there was little concern for cytotoxicity to cells derived from human and toxicity to mitochondria.

Based on the above, it was judged that there is no safety concerns related to secondary pharmacological effects caused by the administration of ensitrelvir fumaric acid at clinical dosing regimen of 375/125 mg.

## 4.3.1.3. Safety pharmacology

The safety pharmacology evaluation of ensitrelvir fumaric acid comprises three GLP-compliant studies and included CNS and cardiovascular/respiratory system (hERG and telemetry/blood gas analysis) studies.

## Central nervous system

In study S-217622-SF-035-L, the effects of ensitrelvir on CNS was studied in Sprague Dawley rats. 6 animals/dose group (at doses 0 (control), 20, 100, or 1000 mg/kg) were administered with a single oral dose of ensitrelvir. No abnormal signs with regards to general behaviour and neurobehavioral function were detected in animals at doses ranging from at 20 and 100 mg/kg. However, at a dose of 1000 mg/kg significant changes in decrease in urine quantity and number of faecal pellets were observed. The decrease in urine quantity was statistically significant between 0h and 1 hour after dosing. Also, a decrease in total number of faecal pellets was noted between 0 and 8h after dosing. No CNS effects were observed in any of the dosing groups and a NOEL was set to 1000 mg/kg which is equivalent to a plasma exposure (Cmax) of 294 µg/mL corresponding to a margin of x10.5 to that of clinical exposure.

## Cardiovascular/respiratory system

Electrophysiological effects of ensitrelvir fumaric acid on hERG channel current stably expressed in CHO cells was evaluated. The concentrations tested were (0), 10, 30, 100 µmol/L corresponded to 0, 5.32,

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

16.0, and 53.2 µg/mL, respectively. DMSO at 0,5 % was used as negative control and the selective inhibitor of the rapidly activating component of delayed rectifier potassium current (IKr), was used as positive control at a concentration of 0.1 μmol/L. The compensated suppression rates for tail peak currents were 8.92%, 16.16%, and 38.98% for each tested concentration exhibiting dose dependency. It is noted however, that the clinical Cmax was 42.1 μg/mL at the highest dose at day 5, resulting in a low margin at the highest ensitrelvir concentration in this assay, i.e. 100 µmol/L/53.2 µg/mL.

The effects of ensitrelvir fumaric acid, orally administered as a single dose, on cardiovascular and respiratory systems was evaluated in conscious male monkeys using a telemetry system. At the lowest dose of 10 mg/kg no findings were observed. At 50 and 150 mg/kg increases in heart rates were noted in 3 animals. The toxicokinetic analysis at the highest dose tested, i.e. 150 mg/kg, exhibited a Cmax of 326 µg/mL and a AUC0-24hr of 6190 µg·hr/mL. The plasma exposure at (Cmax) at NOEL 10mg/kg for cardiovascular system was 43.8 µg/mL (× 1.6 margin human exposure) and for respiratory system, at NOEL 150 mg/kg, 326 µg/mL (× 11.6).

No findings on blood pressure, blood gases and ECG parameters (PR interval, QRS duration, QT interval, and corrected QT interval [QTc]) were observed up to the highest dose of 150 mg/kg. No findings on respiratory system were observed at any dose.

## 4.3.2. Pharmacokinetics

## 4.3.2.1. Absorption

Absorption of ensitrelvir was evaluated in three studies after single oral administration of [ 14 C]- ensitrelvir fumaric acid in rat and monkey, and in a PK/PD study in mice. Only one oral dose level was used in rats and monkeys and only males were dosed thus preventing evaluation of doseproportionality in these species. In the PK/PD study in mice only females were evaluated and five oral dose levels were evaluated.

Rats were dosed with a single oral administration (2mg/kg) and a single intravenous administration (1mg/kg) of radiolabelled and non-radioactive ensitrelvir fumaric acid, respectively, in rats under nonfasting conditions to evaluate pharmacokinetic parameters.

After oral administration of [ 14 C]-ensitrelvir fumaric acid, the concentration of radioactivity (ensitrelvir equivalent) and concentration of ensitrelvir in plasma reached Cmax 2320±450 ng/mL at 2.00 hours and 2220±590 ng/mL 1.75±0.50 hours, respectively. The terminal half-life value was 5.24±0.18 and 3.61±0.56 hours, respectively. The AUC0-inf ratio to radioactivity (21100 ± 600 ng·hr/mL and 17800±1500) was 84.3 %, indicating that most of the radioactivity in plasma was unchanged ensitrelvir fumaric acid. The concentration of radioactivity in blood was also measured, resulting in a Cmax of 1490±240 ng/mL at 2.00 hours. The terminal half-life in blood was 4.31±0.89 hours and was lower at the all the measured time points spanning 0,5-48 hours.

The pharmacokinetic parameters for ensitrelvir in plasma were estimated after a single intravenous administration of ensitrelvir fumaric acid at 1 mg/kg. The AUC0-inf was 10400 ng·hr/mL and the bioavailability was calculated to 85,5%, indicating a high oral absorption.

Plasma concentration of radioactivity and ensitrelvir fumaric acid was also studies in monkeys at 2 mg/kg as single dose under non-fasting condition (study S-217622-PF-026-N). Here, the Cmax in plasma as concentration of radioactivity, 4120±360 ng/mL, was reached at 8.00 hours. The AUC0-inf ratio of ensitrelvir to radioactivity was calculated to 82.8%, almost equal to the corresponding value in rats of 84.3 %. As in rats, the concentration of radioactivity in blood decreased with time and in parallel with that in plasma, indicating that no ensitrelvir related compounds remained in the blood compartment.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Pharmacokinetic analysis was also conducted in study S-217622-EB-018-N (see also section 4.3.1.1 in vivo primary pharmacodynamics.). Herein, ensitrelvir fumaric acid was administered as single dose 2, 8, 16, 32, or 64 mg/kg to female BALB/c mice after intranasal inoculation. In plasma, a dose proportional increase Cmax values of ensitrelvir was observed. AUC0-24hr values of ensitrelvir increased almost dose-proportionally over the tested doses, indicative of a linear PK response over the tested doses. It is however noted that C24hr (ng/mL) is higher at 8 mg/kg than for 16 mg/kg.

## 4.3.2.2. Distribution

The applicant has conducted in vitro studies evaluating protein binding and distribution to blood cells of ensitrelvir. In addition, in vivo distribution was evaluated in rats and monkeys.

## In vitro

In vitro protein binding ratios of [ 14 C]-ensitrelvir was evaluated, at 0.5, 5, or 50 µg/mL, in sera from rats, New Zealand white rabbits and cynomolgus monkeys. Overall, the protein binding ratio was high in all tested species, including human, ranging from 97.4%-98.9%, and did not exhibit species differences and concentration dependent changes. The main binding protein in human serum was human serum albumin (HSA). This evaluation was only performed in human samples.

Blood cell distribution of [ 14 C]-ensitrelvir was calculated to be 5.6% to 12.9% in rats and 9.0% to 10.4% in monkeys. Compared to human, 5.2% to 8.0%, the distribution ratio to blood cells did not show any species differences.

## In vivo

Pigmented Long Evans and Sprague Dawley rats were orally administered with a single dose of 2mg/kg [ 14 C]-ensitrelvir under non-fasting condition. Tissue distribution was investigated by means of quantitative whole-body autoradiography (QWBA). Pharmacokinetic parameters were also estimated and in pigmented rats a Cmax of 1924 ng/g was obtained at 4 hours, compared to a Cmax of 2320 at 2 hours in male Sprague Dawley rats. Maximum concentration of radioactivity was reached at 4 hours post-dose and had a wide distribution across the body. The highest concentration of radioactivity was detected in liver (3.33 µg/g), ciliary body/iris (1.15 µg/g), tooth pulp (0.989 µg/g) and uveal tract (0.942 µg/g). At this time-point, radioactivity concentrations were either BLQ or ND in brain, spinal cord, bone surface and lens of the eye. At 72 hours post-dose the radioactivity concentration had reached BLQ levels throughout the body with the exception of large and small intestine, liver, eye (ciliary body/iris and uveal tract) and nasal mucosa. In pigmented structures of the eye and in pigmented skin the concentration of radioactivity was close or below LOQ at 24 and 336h post-dose, respectively, indicating that there is a somewhat higher affinity to pigmented tissues but seems unlikely to remain in tissues beyond 336 hours.

Placental permeability was studied in pregnant female Sprague Dawley rats at 2 mg/kg on gestation Day 17 under non fasting conditions administered orally with [ 14 C]-ensitrelvir fumaric acid under non fasting conditions. Tissue distribution of [ 14 C]-ensitrelvir fumaric acid in dams and foetuses was investigated by means of QWBA up to 48 hours. In dams and foetuses, the highest radioactivity concentration in tissues was reached at 4h post dose. The concentration of radioactivity was lower in foetal tissues than in the dams and decreased to BLQ by 24 hours post-dose. The data indicates that ensitrelvir passes the placenta but does probably not remain in tissues. Aspects of reproductive toxicity are further discussed in toxicological section.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The in vivo distribution to blood cells after a single oral administration of ensitrelvir fumaric acid was studied in rats and monkeys (studies S-217622-PF-020-N and S-217622-PF-026-N) showing distribution ratios of radioactivity for rats and monkeys of 10.3% to 25.7% and 1.1% to 10.2%, respectively. As presented under the section for absorption, the radioactivity in blood decreased with time and in parallel with that in plasma for both species.

## 4.3.2.3. Metabolism

## In vitro

In vitro metabolism of ensitrelvir was evaluated in cryopreserved hepatocytes from rat, monkey and humans. [ 14 C]-ensitrelvir was incubated at 5 µmol/L with hepatocytes. In human, five metabolites (M2-M6) of ensitrelvir were identified. Three of these (M4-M6) were identified in both rat and monkey. M2 and M3 were identified in monkey but not in rat. M2, M4 and M5 were identified by means radioactivity in the sample, whereas the metabolite M3 and M6 were detected on LC/MS chromatograms without radioactive peak. According to the structural composition of these two metabolites the radiolabelled carbon is present, but no explanation is given in the Applicants Pharmacokinetic Written Summary why no radioactivity is detected. The applicant is asked to clarify this discrepancy (OC).

In vitro, M1 was detected in rat and monkey but not in human. Unchanged ensitrelvir was the major radioactive component in human, rat and monkey with a composition of 75.1-79.2, 72.3 and 79.8%, respectively.

The proposed main metabolic pathways in vitro in are glucuronidation, oxidation, and demethylation.

## In vivo

In rats, at 2,8- and 24-hours post-dose, unchanged ensitrelvir accounted for 43.6% to 85.1% followed by M6 (0.7% to 3.7%) and M4 (≤ 1.6%). At 48 hours post-dose unchanged ensitrelvir in faeces and urine was 36.7% and 0.3 % of the administered radioactivity. In bile, 48 hours post-dose unchanged ensitrelvir comprised 4.5% of the administered radioactivity whereas the main metabolites were M4 (6.0 %) and M6 (5.7%).

In rabbits, the corresponding figure for unchanged ensitrelvir at 4-24 hours post-dose was 74.2% to 86.0% of plasma radioactivity followed by M6 constituting 1.5% of plasma radioactivity. In rabbits, no determination of radioactivity in faeces, urine or bile was carried out.

In monkeys, unchanged Ensitrelvir was the major component in plasma comprising 54.6% to 91.0% of plasma radioactivity, at time points 4, 24 and 48 hours. This was followed by M6 (0.7% to 8.0%) and M4 (1.0% to 1.7%). In urine at 72h post dose and faeces at 96 h post-dose, unchanged ensitrelvir comprised 0.8% and 24.5% of administered radioactivity respectively. In bile, 72 hours post-dose, unchanged ensitrelvir was detected at 4.6% of the administered radioactivity. Main metabolites detected in bile were M2 (12.2%), M11 (4.0 %), M4 (3.5%) and M9 (3.3%)

In conclusion, no major metabolites were detected in the tested species. In addition, in plasma the same pattern could be discerned in the tested non-clinical species compared to human with respects to the plasma radioactivity derived from unchanged ensitrelvir and the M6 metabolite.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.3.2.4. Excretion

Excretion studies were conducted in rats and monkeys. In addition, excretion into milk was studied in nursing rats. In all studies evaluating excretion the animals, [ 14 C]-ensitrelvir fumaric acid was orally administered under a non-fasting condition.

Except for the study evaluating excretion into milk of ensitrelvir, no dedicated excretion studies were performed but excretion was evaluated in other in studies S-217622-PF-020-N and S-217622-PF-026N. These studies made used of intact and bile duct-cannulated rats and monkeys, respectively. In both species the predominant excretion route of ensitrelvir was faeces via bile, but also urine following oral administration. The data indicates similar excretion routes in monkeys and rats.

Excretion into milk showed that the highest radioactivity concentration in milk was reached at 4 hours post dose (1750±250 ng/mL) and was higher than that in plasma at that time point (1430±310 ng/mL). The parallel decrease of radioactivity concentration in milk and plasma indicates that ensitrelvir, and related compounds, did not remain in milk. At 48 hours, the radioactivity concentration in milk and plasma was 11.1±5.1 ng/mL and 5.00±5.49, respectively. The elimination half-life (t1/2) was 5.95 hours

## 4.3.2.5. Other pharmacokinetic studies

N/A

## 4.4. Toxicology

## 4.4.1. Single-dose toxicity

Designated single-dose toxicity studies to evaluate the acute toxicity of ensitrelvir fumaric acid were not conducted but acute toxicity was evaluated based on the results of exploratory single-dose TK studies in rats and monkeys, and the results on the first day of dosing in 2-week repeat-dose oral toxicity studies in rats and monkeys, and in vivo micronucleus study in rats. In the rat 2-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study and micronucleus study, no deaths or moribund animals were observed on the first day of dosing up to the maximum doses of 1000 and 2000 mg/kg, respectively. In the 2week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys, no deaths or moribund animals were observed on the first day of dosing up to the highest dose of 1000 mg/kg (male) or 300 mg/kg (female) but vomiting and a decrease of food consumption were observed in males. The approximate lethal doses for rats and monkeys were determined to be greater than 2000 and 1000 mg/kg, respectively.

## 4.4.2. Repeat-dose toxicity

Rat

In the 2-week (dose levels: 20, 100, and 1000 mg/kg/day) and 4-week (dose levels: 20, 50, and 1000 mg/kg/day) repeat-dose oral toxicity studies in the rat, no adverse effects were observed up to 1000 mg/kg/day, and the NOAEL was identified at 1000 mg/kg/day in both studies. Plasma exposure (Cmax and AUC0-24hr) of ensitrelvir at the NOAEL in the 4-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study were 9 to 13 times and 8 to 10 times higher, respectively, than the clinical dosing regimen of ensitrelvir at 375/125 mg.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Monkey

In the 2-week oral toxicity study in monkeys (dose levels for males: 10, 50, or 1000/300/100 mg/kg/day [dose level reduction from 1000 to 300 mg/kg/day on Day 3, and from 300 to 100 mg/kg/day on Day 8 of dosing], dose levels for females: 10, 50, or 300/100 mg/kg/day [dose level reduction from 300 to 100 mg/kg/day on Day 9 of dosing]), and in the 4-week oral toxicity study in monkeys (dose levels: 3, 10, and 30 mg/kg/day), adverse effects attributable to ensitrelvir fumaric acid included death/moribundity, vomiting, decrease in body weight and food consumption, decreased erythrocyte parameters and platelet counts, and inflammatory changes in multiple organs accompanied by changes in blood inflammatory markers. Plasma exposures (Cmax and AUC0-24hr) of ensitrelvir at the NOAEL (10 mg/kg/day) in the 4-week oral toxicity study in monkeys were 2.7 to 3.4 times and 2.3 to 3.6 times higher than plasma exposures (Cmax: 28.1 µg/mL, AUC0-τ: 518.3 µg·hr/mL) in the clinical dosing regimen of ensitrelvir at 375/125 mg, respectively.

In the 2-week study death or moribundity occurred in males administered at a dose level of 1000 mg/kg/day and at a reduced dose level (from 1000 mg/kg/day) of 300 mg/kg/day, and in a female at a dose of 300 mg/kg/day. In addition, there were animals requiring veterinary care due to a continuous reduction of food consumption at doses of 1000/300/100 mg/kg/day (male) and 300/100 mg/kg/day (female) in the 2-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys, and 30 mg/kg/day of the 4-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys.

## 4.4.3. Genotoxicity

Ensitrelvir fumaric acid has no genotoxic potential based on negative results in the bacterial reverse mutation test, the micronucleus test with the human lymphoblast cell line, or the in vivo micronucleus test in the rat.

## 4.4.4. Carcinogenicity

Carcinogenicity studies were not conducted with ensitrelvir fumaric acid. The intended clinical use of ensitrelvir fumaric acid is 5 day-administration and ensitrelvir fumaric acid has no concern for genotoxicity, therefore the carcinogenic potential is considered to be low.

## 4.4.5. Developmental and reproductive toxicity

In the study on fertility and early embryonic development in rats (dose levels: 20, 60, and 1000 mg/kg/day), no adverse effects were observed. The NOAEL for general toxicity and reproductive function in parental animals and early embryonic development in this study was 1000 mg/kg/day.

In the embryo-foetal development study in the rat (dose levels: 20, 60, and 1000 mg/kg/day), no embryo-foetal malformations or lethality were observed at doses up to 1000 mg/kg/day, and there were no effects on reproductive function of the dams. Decreased food consumption and body weight gain were noted in dams at 1000 mg/kg/day, and slight foetal growth retardation (low foetal body weight and decreased number of ossified sternebrae) were noted in foetuses at 1000 mg/kg/day (associated with maternal adverse effects such as decreased food consumption up to 25% accompanied by decreased body weight gain in the order of 30% in the first days and 11% at late gestation). High frequency of short supernumerary rib was observed at 1000 mg/kg/day; this is considered as a skeletal variation and not a malformation and is known to disappear with growth after birth. The NOAEL was 60 mg/kg/day for embryo-foetal development and maternal general toxicity.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In the embryo-foetal development study in rabbits (dose levels: 30, 100, and 300 mg/kg/day), decreased body weight (up to 6% at 100 mg/kg/day and 8% at 300 mg/kg/day, compared to the control), decreased food consumption (up to 62% and 94% in the high-dose and mid-dose, respectively) accompanied by decreased body-weight gain (or even weight-loss at a magnitude of up to -200 g at early gestation) were observed in the dams. Skeletal abnormalities (abnormalities in axial skeleton morphology such as vertebrae, ribs, and sternebrae) and short tails in the foetus were observed at 100 mg/kg/day and above. In addition, a low embryo-foetal viability was observed at a dose of 300 mg/kg/day, and abortion was observed in 1 dam showing a marked decrease in food consumption at a dose of 100 mg/kg/day. The decrease in food consumption was more prominent at the first half of gestation. The effect on absolute body weight was more modest but effects on bodyweight gain was more prominent and was manifested as body-weight loss reflecting the severe decrease in food-consumption. From these results, the NOAEL for embryo-foetal development and maternal toxicities was 30 mg/kg/day.

To investigate the effects of maternal toxicity and short-term administration (3 to 4 days) of 300 mg/kg/day during the divided organogenesis period additional studies were conducted in the rabbit. Again, ensitrelvir fumaric acid induced decreases in maternal body weight and food consumption at 300 mg/kg/day accompanied by axial skeletal malformations and embryo-foetal lethality. The frequency of occurrence was higher following administration in the early stage of organogenesis. Ensitrelvir fumaric acid under short-term administration in the rabbit is concluded to induce embryo-foetal lethality at early phase of the organogenesis period and foetal growth retardation at almost all phase of the organogenesis period, and axial skeletal malformations from early to middle of the organogenesis period.

In the study for pre- and postnatal developmental and maternal function in rats (dose levels: 20, 60, and 1000 mg/kg/day), decreased body weight gain, trend of low body weight and decreased food consumption were observed in the dams at 1000 mg/kg/day. Total litter loss was also observed in 5 of 20 animals in the early stage of lactation. The following changes were observed at 1000 mg/kg/day: low numbers of newborns, low viability of pups on Days 0 and 4 after birth, low body weight of pups throughout lactation period, low completion rate of eyelids opening and delayed sexual development. The NOAEL for maternal toxicity and pre- and postnatal development in pups was 60 mg/kg/day.

## 4.4.7. Local tolerance

Since the intended therapeutic route is oral, no designated local tolerance study of ensitrelvir fumaric acid was conducted.

## 4.4.8. Other toxicity studies

Phototoxicity

Ensitrelvir fumaric acid did not show absorption at wavelengths ranging from 400 to 700 nm but exhibited absorption in the range of 290 to 700 nm. Therefore, to assess the phototoxicity of ensitrelvir fumaric acid a Neutral Red-Uptake Phototoxicity Test in 3T3 Mouse Fibroblasts was performed at concentrations up to 100 µg/mL. It was concluded that ensitrelvir fumaric acid is negative for phototoxicity in BALB/c 3T3 cells and therefore the risk of phototoxicity in humans is considered to be low.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Mechanistic Studies for HDL- Cholesterol Reduction

Mechanistic studies were conducted to further investigate the HDL-cholesterol reduction that was observed in the clinical Phase 1 study of ensitrelvir fumaric acid.

The results of in vitro studies suggested ensitrelvir inhibited LCAT activity in monkey plasma but not the cholesterol efflux activity by HDL.

Ensitrelvir fumaric acid was orally administered to cynomolgus monkeys repeatedly for 10 days (dose level: 10 mg/kg/day). From Day 2, blood total cholesterol decreased accompanied by decreased HDL cholesterol and LDL cholesterol, but no changes were observed in total cholesterol and its metabolite total bile acid in faeces and bile, suggesting that ensitrelvir did not affect cholesterol excretion.
