Considering the above data from Phase I and Phase II, ensitrelvir is not expected to pose a risk to the environment.

A bioaccumulation potential is not indicated based on the log KOW &lt;4.5. A definitive PBT/vPvB assessment is not required.

## 4.5. Overall discussion and conclusions on non-clinical aspects

## 4.5.1. Discussion

Pharmacology Ensitrelvir is an inhibitor of the severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 (SARS-CoV-2) main protease (M pro , also known as 3CL pro or nsp5 protease), which is essential for the processing of the polyprotein encoded by the SARS-CoV-2 genome as well as the replication of the virus. In contrast to Paxlovid, which includes nirmatrelvir and ritonavir as a PK enhancer, ensitrelvir reaches antiviral concentrations without further PK enhancement.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## In vivo PD studies

The in vivo antiviral effects of ensitrelvir were investigated in mouse models of SARS-CoV-2 infection. The Applicant has submitted five primary pharmacodynamic studies. In three of the studies, ensitrelvir was orally administered to mice inoculated intranasally with either the SARS-CoV-2 Gamma variant (Pango lineage P.1: hCoV-19/Japan/TY7-501/2021 strain) or the lethal MA-P10 strain (mouse-adapted hCoV-19/Japan/TY/WK-521/2020). In two studies evaluating the prophylactic effects of ensitrelvir, the mode of administration was subcutaneous. All studies were performed in female BALB/c mice.

Two different short-term dosing regimens were evaluated (reports S-217622-EB-018-N and S-217622EB-081-N). For analysis of virus titre reduction in vivo, in a manner similar to a clinical post-exposure prophylaxis setting, doses of 0.5, 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, and 64 mg/kg/dose of ensitrelvir fumaric acid were orally administered either QD, BID, or QID for 1 day, starting directly after intranasal infection with the human SARS-CoV-2 strain. This model is only able to analyse the effect on virus replication in relation to a given plasma drug exposure, since infection of mice with human non-adapted SARS-CoV2 strains does not result in COVID-like symptoms and is therefore not an adequate disease model. Lung virus titres decreased 24 hours after the first administration with all dosing regimens in a dosedependent manner. While a four-times-daily treatment regimen resulted in a significant virus titre reduction at a cumulative daily dose of 16 mg/kg/day, a similar reduction was achieved with a single dose of 4 mg/kg/day administered at once. This highlights the importance of early achievement of plasma exposure within an antiviral effective range. Plasma concentrations after a single administration were measured and increased with increasing dose. Tmax occurred early, between 1 and 2 hours after administration. In this model, in which the PK/PD relationship was simulated by nonparametric superposition and a non-compartmental method, the coefficients of determination (r²) of Cmax/PA-EC50 (mouse protein-adjusted EC50), AUC0-24hr/PA-EC50, TimeHigh (1 × PA-EC50; representing the total time above the target plasma concentration), and C24hr/PA-EC50 against lung virus titre reduction 24 hours after the first administration were 0.856, 0.793, 0.266, and 0.114, respectively. In conclusion, Cmax and AUC0-24hr were confirmed as PK parameters that predicted the decrease in lung virus titre 24 hours after the first dose.

In a second study (report S-217622-EB-081-N) designed to analyse the efficacy of delayed treatment, treatment was evaluated in the same mouse model with the same virus strain. Ensitrelvir fumaric acid was orally administered starting 24 hours after virus inoculation at doses of 8, 16, 32, and 64 mg/kg BID (every 12 hours) and TID (every 8 hours) for 2 days, and at 16, 32, and 64 mg/kg QD for 2 days. Additionally, doses of 32 and 64 mg/kg QD were administered for 1 day. Virus titres in the lungs were measured 24 and 48 hours after the first administration. Ensitrelvir fumaric acid treatment significantly reduced lung virus titres in a dose-dependent manner for all doses and administration frequencies except QD dosing for a single day. Parameters of AUC0-48hr/PA-EC50, C48hr/PA-EC50, and TimeHigh (10 × PA-EC50 and 5 × PA-EC50) showed good correlation with the reduction in lung virus titre 48 hours after the first administration of ensitrelvir fumaric acid. From the PK/PD analysis in this mouse model, the C48hr/PA-EC50 values required to reduce the lung virus titre 48 hours after the initial administration of ensitrelvir fumaric acid by 1 log10, 2 log10, and 3 log10 TCID50/mL were 0.769, 3.78, and 14.7, respectively. When calculated using the mouse PA-EC50 (2090 ng/mL [3.93 μmol/L]) obtained from VeroE6/TMPRSS2 cells, the Ctrough values required to reduce lung virus titres by 1 log10, 2 log10, and 3

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

log10 TCID50/mL were 1610, 7900, and 30,700 ng/mL, respectively. In addition, when calculated using the human PA-EC50 (1610 ng/mL [3.02 μmol/L]) obtained from VeroE6/TMPRSS2 cells, the plasma concentrations in humans required to reduce virus titre by 1 log10, 2 log10, and 3 log10 TCID50/mL were estimated to be 1240, 6090, and 23,700 ng/mL, respectively. Both mouse studies that analysed the human SARS-CoV-2 Gamma variant have limitations as animal disease models for in vivo pharmacodynamics. Therefore, the Applicant conducted a study (report S217622-EB-099-N) in female BALB/c mice intranasally infected with the lethal mouse-adapted SARSCoV-2 MA-P10 strain (mouse-adapted based on hCoV-19/Japan/TY/WK-521/2020, ancestral Pango A). Ensitrelvir fumaric acid was administered 24 hours after infection at oral doses of 4, 8, 16, or 32 mg/kg BID or vehicle for 5 days, and the animals were observed for a further 8 days. Survival was significantly improved in all dose groups, whereas body weight improved only at doses ≥8 mg/kg BID. In this study, no PK analysis was included to enable a PK/PD analysis and determination of EC90. Since body-weight changes and survival are only surrogate parameters for therapeutic effect, additional analysis of virus titres and histological evaluation of lung tissue is considered crucial for a reliable evaluation of the antiviral efficacy of ensitrelvir in an established animal disease model for COVID-19. These data were, however, not provided by the Applicant and this is considered a shortcoming of the study. The same mouse model was used for analysis of prophylactic treatment with single subcutaneous doses of 32, 64, 96, or 128 mg/kg administered 24 hours before infection. Survival time and bodyweight changes were significantly improved at doses of ≥64 mg/kg SC. The mean plasma concentrations of ensitrelvir 24 hours post-dose at 32, 64, 96, and 128 mg/kg were 1740, 2990, 3110, and 3370 ng/mL, respectively. The minimum plasma concentration of ensitrelvir associated with significant prophylactic efficacy was 2990 ng/mL (C24hr at 64 mg/kg) at the time of SARS-CoV-2 infection. In a second prophylactic study using the lethal mouse-adapted strain, ensitrelvir fumaric acid was administered to BALB/c mice as a single subcutaneous dose of 64 or 128 mg/kg 24 hours before infection. Lung viral titres peaked 2 days after infection and subsequently decreased in all groups, including the vehicle group, until Day 9. A significant dose-dependent reduction in viral titres was observed only 1 day after infection for both dose levels. Only administration of 128 mg/kg resulted in a sustained significant reduction in viral titres until Day 4 after infection. These findings support the previously identified minimum effective plasma concentration of 2990 ng/mL at the time of infection. The C24hr values achieved with the clinical dosing regimen (375/125 mg) were 14,000 to 17,700 ng/mL. Considering the approximately threefold lower EC50 for ensitrelvir fumaric acid in the mouseadapted MA-P10 strain than in most human variants, together with a similar potency shift in mouse and human serum, the results of this study support the clinical dosing regimen.

## Secondary pharmacology

The applicant has submitted four studies evaluating the secondary pharmacodynamics of ensitrelvir fumaric acid. A screening assay covering 108 molecular targets (49 receptors, 19 ion channels, 6 transporters, 31 enzymes, and 3 kinases) was conducted at a concentration of 100 µM ensitrelvir. For molecular targets showing more than 50% inhibition or stimulation, the IC50 values were determined. The IC50 values for inhibition of the enzyme activities of PDE4A1A, PDE4B1, PDE4C1, and PDE4D2 were 63.2, 69.1, 75.7, and 73.4 μmol/L, respectively. No effects were observed on other receptors, ion channels, transporters, or enzymes at concentrations up to 100 μmol/L (53.2 μg/mL). The IC50 value for inhibition of adenosine uptake via the adenosine transporter was 1.43 μmol/L.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The expected unbound Cmax following administration of ensitrelvir 375/125 mg is 1.2 μmol/L. Thus, the IC50 values for all identified targets, with the exception of the adenosine transporter, were substantially higher (53- to 63-fold) than the unbound Cmax. The IC50 value for the adenosine transporter was 1.43 μmol/L, corresponding to an exposure margin of approximately 1.2-fold relative to the unbound concentration at the human Cmax of 28.1 μg/mL. Inhibition of adenosine transport can cause vasodilation by increasing plasma adenosine concentrations. Effects on the central nervous system (reduced sleep induction), cardiovascular system (decreased blood pressure and heart rate), immune system (anti-inflammatory effects), and platelet aggregation have been reported for adenosine uptake inhibitors (Deussen, 1999). However, corresponding off-target effects were not observed in the telemetry study in monkeys or in the pivotal repeat-dose toxicity studies. Effects on coagulation were observed only in one developmental and reproductive toxicity (DART) study in rabbits, in which reduced PT and APTT values were reported. Despite the low exposure margin for adenosine transporter inhibition, no findings related to cardiovascular or central nervous system endpoints were observed. Potential changes suggestive of reduced glomerular filtration rate were observed in moribund females in the 2-week repeat-dose toxicity study in monkeys; however, no pathological kidney lesions were identified. These findings were considered secondary to the deteriorating health status of these animals. No other renal effects potentially related to increased adenosine levels were observed in rats or in the 4-week repeat-dose toxicity study in monkeys. Similarly, a stress-related increase in blood glucose was observed in one moribund female monkey in the 2-week repeat-dose toxicity study. Importantly, no clinical safety concerns potentially related to adenosine transporter inhibition were identified in humans. In addition, the in vivo inhibitory effect of ensitrelvir on adenosine uptake was found to be subclinical at concentrations in the range of the in vitro IC50. By comparison, the clinical exposure to dipyridamole, an inhibitor of adenosine uptake, is approximately 15- to 61-fold higher than its respective in vitro IC50. The concomitant use of ensitrelvir and dipyridamole, despite the latter having a narrow therapeutic range, is considered to be of minor clinical relevance, and a specific warning is not considered necessary. The effect of ensitrelvir fumaric acid on plasma LCAT (lecithin:cholesterol acyltransferase) activity was also investigated. The data showed that ensitrelvir inhibited LCAT activity but did not affect HDLmediated cholesterol efflux (see Section 4.4.8). To distinguish true antiviral effects from general cytotoxicity, CC50 values for ensitrelvir fumaric acid were determined in nine cell lines. All CC50 values exceeded 72 μg/mL (135 μmol/L). These data covered all relevant cell lines and concentration ranges used in the in vitro studies, indicating that cytotoxicity was not a concern. Although the mechanism of action of ensitrelvir involves inhibition of the nsp5 protease (M pro ) rather than inhibition of nucleoside reverse transcriptase, for which mitochondrial toxicity is a known class effect, the Applicant investigated the potential effects of ensitrelvir on mitochondrial function. HepG2 cells cultured in glucose- or galactose-containing medium were incubated with ensitrelvir at concentrations ranging from 0.096 to 300 µg/mL for 2 or 14 days. HepG2 cells are proliferative cells that predominantly generate intracellular ATP through glycolysis and are therefore relatively resistant to mitochondrial toxicants in glucose-containing medium. Replacement of glucose with galactose renders these cells more sensitive to mitochondrial dysfunction because ATP production becomes more

dependent on mitochondrial oxidative phosphorylation. Accordingly, mitochondrial toxicity can be identified by comparing cytotoxic effects in cells cultured in glucose-containing versus galactosecontaining medium.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

No differences in ATP levels, the primary endpoint for mitochondrial function, were observed between the two culture conditions. The CC50 values for ensitrelvir were 287 and 294 µg/mL after 2 days of incubation and 100 and 97.9 µg/mL after 14 days of incubation in glucose- and galactose-containing media, respectively. Oligomycin and chloramphenicol were included as positive controls. No evidence of mitochondrial toxicity was identified following either short- or long-term exposure.

Furthermore, no clear signals suggestive of mitochondrial toxicity were identified in the non-clinical toxicity programme or in the clinical safety database. A reduction in HDL levels could potentially be considered a signal related to lipodystrophy, a condition for which NRTI-induced mitochondrial toxicity has been suggested as a contributing factor. Mitochondrial toxicity was assessed in accordance with the FDA Guidance for Industry Antiviral Product Development: Conducting and Submitting Virology Studies to the Agency (June 2006).

As the two mitochondrial toxicity assays were conducted primarily to fulfil regulatory requirements for an FDA NDA rather than in response to mechanistic concerns or identified safety signals, the limitations of these assays were considered of secondary importance by the CHMP.

## Safety Pharmacology

The safety pharmacology evaluation of ensitrelvir fumaric acid comprises three GLP-compliant studies and included CNS and cardiovascular/respiratory system (hERG and telemetry/blood gas analysis) studies.

## Central nervous system

The effects of ensitrelvir on the CNS were studied in Sprague-Dawley rats. Six animals per dose group (doses of 0 [control], 20, 100, or 1000 mg/kg) were administered a single oral dose of ensitrelvir fumaric acid. No CNS effects were identified up to the highest dose of 1000 mg/kg in a modified FOB analysis in rats, with a safety margin of 10.5-fold relative to a clinical Cmax of 28.1 µg/mL in healthy Asian female volunteers on Day 5 of administration at 375/125 mg. At a dose of 1000 mg/kg, significant decreases in urine quantity and the number of faecal pellets were observed. No CNS effects were observed in any of the dosing groups, and a NOEL was set at 1000 mg/kg, corresponding to a plasma exposure (Cmax) of 294 µg/mL and a margin of 10.5-fold relative to clinical exposure.

## Cardiovascular/respiratory system

The electrophysiological effects of ensitrelvir fumaric acid on hERG channel currents in CHO cells were evaluated at concentrations of 10, 30, and 100 µmol/L, corresponding to 5.32, 16.0, and 53.2 µg/mL, respectively. Compensated suppression rates for tail peak currents were 8.92%, 16.16%, and 38.98% for each tested concentration, demonstrating a dose-dependent effect. It is noted that the clinical Cmax was 42.1 μg/mL at the highest dose on Day 5, resulting in a low margin at the highest ensitrelvir concentration tested in this assay, i.e. 100 µmol/L (53.2 µg/mL). Nevertheless, the compensated suppression rate remained below 50% at the highest tested concentration.

The effects of ensitrelvir fumaric acid, orally administered as a single dose, on the cardiovascular and respiratory systems were evaluated in conscious male monkeys using a telemetry system (Study S217622-SF-033-L). At a dose of 10 mg/kg, no findings were observed. At 50 and 150 mg/kg, increases in heart rate were noted. Toxicokinetic analysis at the highest dose tested (150 mg/kg) exhibited a Cmax of 326 µg/mL and an AUC0-24hr of 6190 µg·hr/mL. The plasma exposure (Cmax) at the NOEL of 10

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

mg/kg for the cardiovascular system was 43.8 µg/mL, corresponding to a 1.6-fold margin relative to human exposure. Although a low safety margin of 1.6 was concluded at the NOEL, the relevance of this finding is limited, as total exposures rather than the pharmacologically relevant free fractions were compared. Ensitrelvir is highly bound to plasma albumin and, in contrast to human plasma, saturation of albumin binding is likely to occur at higher doses in monkeys and may already commence at a single dose of 50 mg/kg, resulting in secondary effects on heart rate (see discussion on monkey repeat-dose toxicity studies). Moreover, no effects on blood pressure were identified that could indicate a vasodilatory off-target effect contributing to reflex tachycardia. Furthermore, no clear signals for cardiovascular effects were identified in the pivotal repeat-dose toxicity studies. Although the applicant did not provide further discussion regarding the low safety margin observed for the identified cardiac effects, the CHMP considered that no cardiovascular safety concern is expected for ensitrelvir fumaric acid administered as monotherapy. For the respiratory system, a NOEL of 150 mg/kg was established, corresponding to a Cmax of 326 µg/mL and a margin of 11.6-fold relative to the clinical total Cmax of 28.1 µg/mL. Overall, the CHMP concluded that, apart from findings related to heart rate and a weak inhibition of hERG channels, no findings regarding blood pressure, blood gases, or ECG parameters (PR interval, QRS duration, QT interval, and corrected QT interval [QTc]) were observed up to the highest dose of

150 mg/kg. No findings related to the respiratory system or CNS were observed at any dose.

## Pharmacokinetics

## Absorption

Absorption of ensitrelvir was evaluated in three studies after single oral administration of [ 14 C]- ensitrelvir fumaric acid in rats and monkeys, and in a PK/PD study in mice. Only one oral dose level was used in rats and monkeys, 2 mg/kg, and only males were dosed thus preventing evaluation of dose-proportionality in these species and comparison between sexes. In the PK/PD study in mice only females were evaluated, but five oral doses were evaluated. In the first study, rats were dosed with a single oral administration (2mg/kg) and a single intravenous administration (1mg/kg). Both radiolabelled and non-radioactive ensitrelvir fumaric acid were administered. After oral administration of [ 14 C]-ensitrelvir fumaric acid, the concentration of radioactivity (ensitrelvir equivalent) and concentration of ensitrelvir in plasma reached Cmax of 2320±450 ng/mL at 2.00 hours and 2220±590 ng/mL 1.75±0.50 hours, respectively. The terminal half-life values were 5.24±0.18 and 3.61±0.56 hours, respectively. The AUC0-inf ratio of unchanged ensitrelvir relative to total radioactivity (21100 ± 600 ng·hr/mL and17800±1500) was 84.3 %, indicating that most of the radioactivity in plasma was unchanged ensitrelvir. The concentration of radioactivity in blood was also measured, resulting in a Cmax of 1490±240 ng/mL at 2.00 hours. The terminal half-life in blood was 4.31±0.89 hours and was lower at the all the measured time points spanning 0.5-48 hours. The pharmacokinetic parameters for ensitrelvir in plasma in rats were estimated after a single intravenous administration of ensitrelvir fumaric acid at 1 mg/kg. The AUC0-inf was 10400 ng·hr/mL and the bioavailability was calculated as 85.5%, indicating a high oral absorption. In the clinical mass balance study QSC206321 an absolute bioavailability of ensitrelvir could not be determined, nevertheless clinical data from this study indicated a relatively high absorption after an orally

administered dose.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Plasma concentration of [ 14 C]-ensitrelvir fumaric acid was also studied in monkeys at 2 mg/kg as single dose under non-fasting conditions. Here, the Cmax in plasma as concentration of radioactivity, 4120±360 ng/mL, was reached at 8.00 hours. The AUC0-inf ratio of ensitrelvir to radioactivity was calculated as 82.8%, almost equal to the corresponding value in rats of 84.3 %. Like rats, the concentration of radioactivity in blood decreased with time and in parallel with that in plasma, indicating that no ensitrelvir related compounds remained in the blood compartment.

Pharmacokinetic analysis was also conducted in mice. Herein, ensitrelvir fumaric acid was administered as single dose 2, 8, 16, 32, or 64 mg/kg to female BALB/c mice after intranasal inoculation. In plasma, there was a dose proportional increase in Cmax values of ensitrelvir. AUC0-24hr values of ensitrelvir increased dose-proportionally over the tested doses, indicative of a linear PK response over the tested doses.

## Distribution

The applicant has conducted in vitro studies evaluating protein binding and distribution to blood cells of ensitrelvir. In addition, in vivo distribution was evaluated in rats and monkeys.

## In vitro

In vitro protein binding ratios of [ 14 C]-ensitrelvir was evaluated in sera from rats, New Zealand white rabbits and cynomolgus monkeys. Overall, the protein binding ratio was high in all tested species, including human, ranging from 97.4%-98.9%, and did not exhibit species differences and concentration dependent changes. The main binding protein in human serum was human serum albumin (HSA).

Blood cell distribution of [ 14 C]-ensitrelvir was calculated to be 5.6% to 12.9% in rats and 9.0% to 10.4% in monkeys. Compared to human, 5.2% to 8.0%, the distribution ratio to blood cells did not show any substantial species differences.

## In vivo

Tissue distribution was investigated by means of quantitative whole-body autoradiography (QWBA). In pigmented rats a Cmax of 1924 ng/g was obtained at 4 hours, compared to a Cmax of 2320 ng/g at 2 hours in male Sprague Dawley rats, and had a wide distribution across the body. The highest concentration of radioactivity was detected in liver, ciliary body/iris, tooth pulp and uveal tract. At this time-point, radioactivity concentrations were either BLQ or ND in brain, spinal cord, bone surface and lens of the eye. At 72 hours post-dose the radioactivity concentration had reached BLQ levels throughout the body with the exception of large and small intestine, liver, eye (ciliary body/iris and uveal tract) and nasal mucosa. In pigmented structures of the eye and in pigmented skin the concentration of radioactivity was close or below LOQ at 24 and 336h post-dose, respectively, indicating a higher affinity to pigmented tissues but seems unlikely to remain in tissues beyond 336 hours.

Placental permeability was studied in pregnant female Sprague Dawley. Tissue distribution of [ 14 C]- ensitrelvir fumaric acid in dams and foetuses was investigated by means of QWBA up to 48 hours. In dams and foetuses, the highest radioactivity concentration in tissues was reached at 4h post dose. The concentration of radioactivity was lower in foetal tissues than in the dams and decreased to BLQ by 24 hours post-dose. The data indicates that ensitrelvir passes the placenta but does probably not remain in tissues. Aspects of reproductive toxicity are further discussed in toxicological section in this AR.

The in vivo distribution to blood cells after a single oral administration of [ 14 C]-ensitrelvir fumaric acid showed distribution ratios of radioactivity in rats and monkeys of 10.3% to 25.7% and 1.1% to 10.2%, respectively. As presented under the section for absorption, the radioactivity in blood decreased with time and in parallel with that in plasma for both species. Taken together, the data indicates that ensitrelvir (and metabolites) does not remain in blood.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Metabolism

## In vitro

In vitro metabolism of [ 14 C]-ensitrelvir was evaluated in cryopreserved hepatocytes from rat, monkey and humans. In human, five metabolites (M2-M6) of ensitrelvir were identified. Four of these (M4-M6) were identified in both rat and monkey. M2 and M3 were identified in monkey but not in rat. M2, M4 and M5 were identified by means of radioactivity in the sample, whereas the metabolite M3 and M6 were detected on LC/MS chromatograms without radioactive peak.

In vitro, M1 was detected in rat and monkey but not in human. Unchanged ensitrelvir was the major radioactive component, in human, rat and monkey ranging from 72.3% to 79.8%.

The proposed main metabolic pathways in vitro are glucuronidation, oxidation, and demethylation.

## In vivo

In vivo metabolism was studied in rat, rabbit and monkey.

In rats, at 2,8- and 24-hours post-dose, unchanged ensitrelvir accounted for 43.6% to 85.1% followed by M6 (0.7% to 3.7%) and M4 (≤ 1.6%) in plasma. In rabbits, the corresponding figure for unchanged ensitrelvir at 4-24 hours post-dose was 74.2% to 86.0% of plasma radioactivity followed by M6 constituting 1.5% of plasma radioactivity.

In monkeys, unchanged Ensitrelvir was the major component in plasma comprising 54.6% to 91.0% of plasma radioactivity. This was followed by M6 (0.7% to 8.0%) and M4 (1.0% to 1.7%).

In human plasma unchanged ensitrelvir accounted for 89.6% based on AUC0-inf and the only detected metabolite was Ensitrelvir indazole 4-Cl (M6) accounting for 4.1% of the total radioactivity. No major metabolites were detected in plasma, urine and faeces in rats, monkeys and humans.

In conclusion, the main metabolic pathways of ensitrelvir were discussed to be demethylation, oxidation, and defluorination, followed by the conjugation of glucuronic acid and glutathione, chlorination, and deindazolation. No major metabolites were detected in the tested species. In plasma the same pattern could be discerned in the tested non-clinical species compared to human with respects to the plasma radioactivity derived from unchanged ensitrelvir and the M6 metabolite.

## Excretion

Excretion studies were conducted in rats and monkeys. Excretion into milk was studied in nursing rats.

Studies S-217622-PF-020-N and S-217622-PF-026-N used intact and bile duct-cannulated rats and monkeys, respectively. In both species the predominant excretion route of ensitrelvir was faeces via bile, but also urine following oral administration, supporting the observations in humans.

Excretion into milk showed that the highest radioactivity concentration in milk was reached at 4 hours post dose (1750±250 ng/mL) and was higher than that in plasma at that time point (1430±310 ng/mL). The parallel decrease of radioactivity concentration in milk and plasma indicates that ensitrelvir, and related compounds, did not remain in milk. At 48 hours, the radioactivity concentration in milk and plasma was 11.1±5.1 ng/mL and 5.00±5.49, respectively. The elimination half-life (t1/2) was 5.95 hours. Despite the rate of clearance in milk there was an increase in death rates in suckling neonates in the PPND study. The excretion of ensitrelvir into milk and a breast-feeding recommendation are reflected in section 4.6 of the SmPC.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Toxicology

## Single-dose toxicity

Dedicated single-dose toxicity studies to evaluate the acute toxicity of ensitrelvir fumaric acid were not conducted, but acute toxicity was evaluated based on the results of exploratory single-dose TK studies in rats and monkeys, the results from the first day of dosing in 2-week repeat-dose oral toxicity studies in rats and monkeys, and an in vivo micronucleus study in rats. In the rat 2-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study and micronucleus study, no deaths or moribund animals were observed on the first day of dosing up to the maximum doses of 1000 and 2000 mg/kg, respectively. In the 2-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys, no deaths or moribund animals were observed on the first day of dosing up to the highest dose of 1000 mg/kg (males) or 300 mg/kg (females), but vomiting and a decrease in food consumption were observed in males. The approximate lethal doses for rats and monkeys were determined to be greater than 2000 and 1000 mg/kg, respectively. When ensitrelvir fumaric acid was administered to rats at a dose of 1000 mg/kg in the 2-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study, the Cmax and AUC0-24hr values (294 to 375 µg/mL and 5400 to 6720 µg·hr/mL) of ensitrelvir in plasma on the first day of dosing were 10- to 13-fold higher than the corresponding Cmax and AUC0-τ values (28.1 µg/mL and 518.3 µg·hr/mL) observed with the clinical dosing regimen of ensitrelvir at 375/125 mg. In addition, when ensitrelvir fumaric acid was administered to male monkeys at a dose of 1000 mg/kg and to female monkeys at a dose of 300 mg/kg in the 2-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys, the Cmax and AUC0-24hr values (433 to 520 µg/mL and 8920 to 10700 µg·hr/mL) on the first day of dosing were 15- to 19 fold and 17- to 21 fold higher, respectively, than Cmax and AUC0-τ values

with the clinical dosing regimen of ensitrelvir at 375/125 mg.

## Repeat-dose toxicity

## Rat

In the 2-week (dose levels: 20, 100, and 1000 mg/kg/day) and 4-week (dose levels: 20, 50, and 1000 mg/kg/day) repeat-dose oral toxicity studies in rats, no adverse effects were observed up to 1000 mg/kg/day, and the NOAEL was identified at 1000 mg/kg/day in both studies. Plasma exposure (Cmax and AUC0-24hr) of ensitrelvir at the NOAEL in the 4-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study was 9 to 13 fold and 8 to 10 fold higher, respectively, than that observed with the clinical dosing regimen of ensitrelvir (375/125 mg).

## Monkey

In the 2-week oral toxicity study in monkeys (dose levels for males: 10, 50, or 1000/300/100 mg/kg/day [dose level reduction from 1000 to 300 mg/kg/day on Day 3, and from 300 to 100 mg/kg/day on Day 8 of dosing], dose levels for females: 10, 50, or 300/100 mg/kg/day [dose level reduction from 300 to 100 mg/kg/day on Day 9 of dosing]), and in the 4-week oral toxicity study in monkeys (dose levels: 3, 10, and 30 mg/kg/day), adverse effects attributable to ensitrelvir fumaric acid included death/moribundity, vomiting, decreased body weight and food consumption, decreased erythrocyte parameters and platelet counts, and inflammatory changes in multiple organs accompanied by changes in blood inflammatory markers. Plasma exposures (Cmax and AUC0-24hr) of ensitrelvir at the NOAEL (10 mg/kg/day) in the 4-week oral toxicity study in monkeys were 2.7 to 3.4 times and 2.3 to 3.6 times higher than plasma exposures (Cmax: 28.1 µg/mL, AUC0-τ: 518.3 µg·hr/mL) in the clinical dosing regimen of ensitrelvir at 375/125 mg, respectively.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In the 2-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys, death or moribundity occurred in males administered 1000 mg/kg/day and at a reduced dose level (from 1000 mg/kg/day) of 300 mg/kg/day, and in one female at a dose of 300 mg/kg/day. In addition, there were animals requiring veterinary care due to a continuous reduction of food consumption at doses of 1000/300/100 mg/kg/day (male) and 300/100 mg/kg/day (female) in the 2-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys, and 30 mg/kg/day of the 4-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys.

These animals had hypoalbuminemia (2.2 to 3.0 g/dL [333 to 455 µmol/L]), and the applicant hypothesized this to be due to decreased food consumption and inflammatory changes. The Cmax of animals with severe toxicity was 211 µg/mL (397 µmol/L) or higher, which was equal to or exceeded the molar concentration of albumin in plasma. The applicant also hypothesized that the toxicity might be more severe in these animals as the unbound plasma concentration of ensitrelvir increased.

Other major findings with ensitrelvir fumaric acid included increased total bilirubin (mainly increased indirect bilirubin) and decreased HDL- and LDL-cholesterol in monkeys. However, none of these changes was associated with histopathological changes and was therefore not considered adverse.

## Causes of death/moribundity

The cause of death/moribundity in the 2 males was concluded to be decreased food consumption and opportunistic infection, caused by the stress-related immunosuppression. The cause of moribundity in the one female was concluded to be decreased food consumption and anaemia.

Cmax and AUC0-24hr values (378 to 404 µg/mL and 7390 to 7810 µg·hr/mL) on Day 14 at 50 mg/kg/day with no mortality/moribundity in the 2-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys were 13 to 14 and 14 to 15 times higher than Cmax and AUC0-τ (28.1 µg/mL and 518.3 µg·hr/mL) with the clinical dosing regimen of ensitrelvir at 375/125 mg, respectively. However, the Cmax was 409 µg/mL (769 µmol/L) or higher when administered at a dose of 300 mg/kg/day or higher, in which moribundity or deaths were observed. The Cmax of ensitrelvir exceeded the plasma albumin concentration during the acclimatization period (3.5 to 4.5 g/dL [530 to 682 µmol/L]).

Furthermore, the moribund animal presented hypoalbuminemia (plasma albumin concentration: 2.8 or 3.0 g/dL [424 or 455 µmol/L]) due to decreased food consumption and inflammatory changes. In animals with death or moribundity, the applicant hypothesized that the toxicity was more severe as the concentration of unbound plasma ensitrelvir increased as described above. The Cmax value with the clinical dosing regimen of ensitrelvir at 375/125 mg was 28.1 µg/mL (52.8 µmol/L), which is substantially lower than the human albumin concentration (4.1 to 5.1 g/dL [621 to 773 µmol/L]). It is therefore unlikely that unbound plasma ensitrelvir concentrations will increase due to saturation of albumin binding in humans.

## Vomiting

Vomiting was observed in males in the 1000/300/100-mg/kg/day group and females in the 300/100-mg/kg/day group in the 2-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys. The frequency of vomiting was higher in both males and females in the first half of the administration period, and the frequency of vomiting tended to decrease as the dose decreased. No vomiting was observed during the 2-week washout period.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In secondary pharmacological evaluation, ensitrelvir showed the inhibitory effects on PDE4A1A, PDE4B1, PDE4C1 and PDE4D2 (IC50 = 63.2 to 75.7 µmol/L). Unbound Cmax value of males in the 1000/300/100-mg/kg/day group and females in the 300/100-mg/kg/day group with vomiting in 2week repeat-dose oral toxicity studies in monkeys was 14 to 17 µmol/L.

The applicant hypothesized that the vomiting observed in the high-dose group of the 2-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys may be due to nonspecific PDE4 inhibition. The ratio between unbound Cmax value in animals with vomiting and the IC50 value for inhibition of each PDE4 subtype were 3.7 to 5.4 times. In humans, the IC50 values of PDE4s (63.2 to 75.7 µmol/L) are 53 to 63 times higher than unbound Cmax value (1.2 µmol/L; calculated from total ensitrelvir concentration [28.1 µg/mL], molecular weight [531.88] and the human protein binding rate [97.7%]) with the clinical dosing regimen of ensitrelvir at 375/125 mg. The CHMP concluded that there was little concern about vomiting induced by nonspecific PDE4 inhibition in humans. Vomiting was not reported as an adverse event in the clinical trials.

## Decreased food consumption

Decreased food consumption was observed at 50 mg/kg/day and above in the 2-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys and at 30 mg/kg/day in the 4-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys. No irritative or degenerative findings in the upper gastrointestinal tract were observed in any of the studies. Decreased food consumption at 30-mg/kg/day group in the 4-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys was observed after Day 10 of dosing and showed reversibility by the drug withdrawal. Decreased food consumption is not considered a critical clinical adverse event, as food intake during treatment can be adequately monitored.

## Decreased erythrocyte parameters

Decreased erythrocyte parameters (erythrocyte count, haemoglobin concentration, and haematocrit value) were observed at 50 mg/kg/day or higher in the 2-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys and at 30 mg/kg/day in the 4-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys. Some animals showed increased reticulocyte counts or hematopoietic reactions in the bone marrow.

The decrease in erythrocyte parameters was considered to be the result of extravascular haemolysis based on the following observations: (1) no obvious change in mean corpuscular volume or mean corpuscular haemoglobin concentration, (2) no haematological and histopathological changes indicating intravascular haemolysis such as increased blood AST and LDH or haemoglobinuria-suspected abnormal urine colour were noted in these animals, and (3) congestion in the spleen with increased spleen weights were noted in these animals.

The decreases in erythrocyte parameters were reversible following withdrawal of ensitrelvir fumaric acid.

## Decreased platelet count

Decreased platelet count was observed in 1 female at 50 mg/kg/day and 1 male at 1000/300/100 mg/kg/day in the 2-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys. In males at 1000/300/100 mg/kg/day, an increase in megakaryocytes was observed in the bone marrow, suggesting decreased platelet count was not mediated by myelosuppression. No decrease in platelet count was observed during the recovery period, indicating reversibility following withdrawal of the drug.

In the 4-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys, no effect on platelet count was observed at doses up to 30 mg/kg/day.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Inflammatory changes in multiple organs

Increased histiocytes and plasma cells in the spleen and lymph nodes, and infiltration of inflammatory cells, mainly mononuclear cells, were noted in the perivascular area of the lung and epididymis, and the oesophagus, lacrimal gland, eyeballs (bulbar conjunctiva), and lamina propria of the gallbladder in animals administered 50 mg/kg/day or above in the 2-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys. In addition, in the 4-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys, inflammatory cell infiltration was observed in the muscular layer of gallbladder and the uterus in one female at a dose of 30 mg/kg/day. Parenchymal cell damage or vascular wall degeneration were not observed in any of the organs in the surviving animals. These results suggested that the inflammatory cell infiltration observed during repeat-dose in monkeys was not due to the response to cytotoxicity or vascular wall degeneration. Inflammatory lesions derived from mainly mononuclear inflammatory cells such as histiocytes, and plasma cells were found in organs which are well known to have inflammatory lesions spontaneously. Since these are considered to be organs where inflammation is likely to occur due to foreign antigens, it is possible that the above inflammatory changes were induced by the enhancement of immune function against foreign antigens by ensitrelvir fumaric acid. In addition, in males in the 50-mg/kg/day administration group, males in the 1000/300/100-mg/kg/day administration group, and females in the 300/100-mg/kg/day administration group in the 2-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys, increased Fbg was observed. This elevation was concluded to be reflected to the above-mentioned inflammatory lesions. All inflammatory changes and inflammatory marker changes observed in the repeat-dose oral toxicity studies in monkeys were reversible following withdrawal of the drug.

## Increased bilirubin

Increased total bilirubin (T.Bil) and direct bilirubin (D.Bil) were observed in animals administered 50 mg/kg/day or higher in the 2-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys and in the 30 mg/kg/day group of the 4-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in monkeys. The increase in D.Bil was smaller than the increase in T.Bil, and the increase in serum bilirubin was therefore mainly attributable to increased indirect bilirubin (I.Bil). However, no other changes suggestive of cholestasis or hepatobiliary disorders were observed in blood chemistry parameters or histopathological examinations in any of the animals. Ensitrelvir has inhibitory activity against organic anion transporting polypeptides (OATP) 1B1 and OATP1B3. Indirect bilirubin produced in the circulation is taken up into the liver via OATP1B1 and OATP1B3, conjugated with glucuronic acid, and subsequently excreted. The unbound Cmax value of ensitrelvir was 7 µmol/L (calculated from the total ensitrelvir concentration [211 µg/mL], molecular weight [531.88], and monkey serum protein binding rate [98.3%]) at doses of 30 mg/kg/day or higher, at which increased indirect bilirubin was observed in the repeat-dose oral toxicity studies in monkeys. This unbound Cmax was close to the IC50 value (13.2 µmol/L) for inhibition of OATP1B1 and exceeded the IC50 value (3.51 µmol/L) for inhibition of OATP1B3. These findings suggest that the increase in serum bilirubin observed in the repeat-dose oral toxicity studies in monkeys was mainly related to OATP inhibition. As discussed above, extravascular haemolysis was suggested at doses of 50 mg/kg/day or higher in the 2-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study and at 30 mg/kg/day in the 4-week repeat-dose oral toxicity study. It is therefore possible that extravascular haemolysis also contributed to the increase in serum bilirubin in animals with decreased erythrocyte parameters.

Assessment report

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Immunotoxicity

The applicant provided a weight of evidence review for immunotoxicity testing and a thorough discussion of the immunosuppressive and inflammatory findings in the repeat-dose toxicity studies and concludes that ensitrelvir possesses a potential for immunotoxicity. However, since all effects recovered after cessation in the animals and no elevation in inflammatory markers or inflammatory symptoms were noticed in the conducted clinical trials, further immunotoxicity testing was not considered necessary by the CHMP.

## Genotoxicity

Ensitrelvir fumaric acid has no genotoxic potential based on negative results in the bacterial reverse mutation test, the micronucleus test with the human lymphoblast cell line, or the in vivo micronucleus test in the rat.

## Carcinogenicity

Carcinogenicity studies were not conducted with ensitrelvir fumaric acid. Given that the intended clinical use of ensitrelvir fumaric acid is limited to 5 days of administration and that ensitrelvir fumaric acid has shown no evidence of genotoxicity, the carcinogenic potential is considered to be low.

## Reproductive Developmental Toxicity

In the study on fertility and early embryonic development in rats (dose levels: 20, 60, and 1000 mg/kg/day), no adverse effects were observed. The NOAEL for general toxicity and reproductive function in parental animals and early embryonic development was 1000 mg/kg/day. Plasma exposure was not measured in this study, however the Cmax and AUC0-24hr values at 1000 mg/kg/day in the 4week repeat-dose oral toxicity study in rats were 9 to 13 fold and 8 to 10 times fold higher, respectively, than those with the clinical dosing regimen at 375/125 mg.

In the embryo-foetal development study in the rat (dose levels: 20, 60, and 1000 mg/kg/day), no embryo-foetal malformations or lethality were observed at doses up to 1000 mg/kg/day, and there were no effects on the reproductive function of the dams. Decreased food consumption and bodyweight gain were noted in dams at 1000 mg/kg/day, and slight foetal growth retardation (low foetal body weight and a decreased number of ossified sternebrae) was noted in foetuses at 1000 mg/kg/day. These findings were associated with maternal toxicity, including decreases in food consumption of up to 25%, accompanied by reductions in body-weight gain of approximately 30% during early gestation and 11% during late gestation. A high frequency of short supernumerary ribs was observed at 1000 mg/kg/day; this is considered a skeletal variation rather than a malformation and is known to disappear with postnatal growth. The NOAEL for embryo-foetal development and maternal toxicity was 60 mg/kg/day. Plasma exposures of ensitrelvir (Cmax and AUC0-24hr) in pregnant rats at the NOAEL were 134 µg/mL and 1990 µg·hr/mL, respectively, which were 4.8- and 3.8-fold higher than those observed with the clinical dosing regimen (375/125 mg).

In the embryo-foetal development study in rabbits (dose levels: 30, 100, and 300 mg/kg/day), decreased body weight (up to 6% at 100 mg/kg/day and 8% at 300 mg/kg/day compared with controls), decreased food consumption (up to 62% and 94% in the mid- and high-dose groups, respectively), and decreased body-weight gain (including body-weight loss of up to 200 g during early gestation) were observed in the dams. Skeletal abnormalities (abnormalities in axial skeletal morphology involving vertebrae, ribs, and sternebrae) and short tails were observed in foetuses at doses of 100 mg/kg/day and above. In addition, reduced embryo-foetal viability was observed at 300 mg/kg/day, and abortion was observed in one dam exhibiting a marked decrease in food consumption at 100 mg/kg/day.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The CHMP considered that abortion and premature birth due to marked decreased food consumption have been reported in pregnant rabbits, suggesting that the abortion observed in this study was due to decreased food consumption due to ensitrelvir fumaric acid administration. The decrease in food consumption was more prominent at the first half of gestation. The effect on absolute body weight was more modest but effects on body-weight gain was more prominent and was manifested as body-weight loss reflecting the severe decrease in food-consumption. From these results, the NOAEL for embryo-foetal development and maternal toxicities was 30 mg/kg/day. Plasma exposures of ensitrelvir (Cmax and AUC0-24hr) in pregnant rabbits at the NOAEL were 68.8 µg/mL and 1260 µg·hr/mL, respectively, which were 2.4 times higher than the exposure with the clinical dosing regimen at 375/125 mg. The dosing period (GD6 to 19) in the rabbit embryo-foetal development study covered the primary organogenesis period from implantation to hard palate closure. Since the clinical dosing regimen of ensitrelvir consists of only 5 days of administration, an additional embryo-foetal development study was conducted in which the organogenesis period was divided into four intervals to assess the effects of shorter-term administration. It has been reported that morphological abnormalities of the foetal axial skeleton in rabbits may be associated with maternal anaemia. To investigate in more detail the relationship between maternal toxicity and foetal axial skeletal malformations, haematology and blood chemistry evaluations were included in the additional study. In both the 2-week repeat-dose oral administration study and the 3- or 4-day repeat-dose administration study in pregnant rabbits, body weight loss and decreased food consumption were observed by the administration of ensitrelvir fumaric acid with a milder effect with short-term administration. Similar changes to the axial skeletal malformations observed in the rabbit embryofoetal development study were also observed after repeat-dose for 3 or 4 days. Axial skeletal malformations were observed by administration from the early to middle stages of organogenesis (GD6 to 15), and the frequency of occurrence was clearly higher in the early stages of organogenesis period (GD6 to 9). No skeletal abnormalities due to ensitrelvir fumaric acid administration were observed during administration in the late stage of organogenesis period (GD16 to 19). In a 2-week repeat-dose oral administration study in pregnant rabbits, decreased erythrocyte parameters were observed in the 100 and 300-mg/kg/day administration groups on GD19. No effects on the above-mentioned erythrocyte parameters were observed after repeat-dose for 3 or 4 days of dosing. From the above findings, effects of ensitrelvir fumaric acid on foetal skeletal development were observed even after short-term administration during early organogenesis and in the absence of detectable effects on erythrocyte parameters. A relationship between the skeletal malformations and effects on erythrocytes could therefore not be established. Maternal toxicity, manifested by bodyweight loss and decreased food consumption, remained evident. In the study for pre- and postnatal developmental and maternal function in rats (dose levels: 20, 60, and 1000 mg/kg/day), decreased body weight gain, trend of low body weight and decreased food consumption were observed in the dams at 1000 mg/kg/day. Total litter loss was also observed in 5 of 20 animals in the early stage of lactation. The following changes were observed at 1000 mg/kg/day: low numbers of newborns, low viability of pups on Days 0 and 4 after birth, low body weight of pups throughout lactation period, low completion rate of eyelids opening and delayed sexual development. The applicant considered these findings to be secondary to maternal toxicity. However, this view was not accepted by the CHMP, as a direct effect of ensitrelvir exposure in utero and via lactation cannot be excluded. Ensitrelvir crosses the placenta and is excreted into milk in rats. Indeed, the toxicological profile of ensitrelvir includes reduced food consumption, decreased body weight, and reduced body-

Assessment report weight gain. In addition, the effects on maternal body weight during gestation and lactation did not exceed approximately 3% and 4%, respectively. The NOAEL for maternal toxicity and pre- and postnatal development in pups was 60 mg/kg/day. Although plasma exposure was not measured in this study, based on the embryo-foetal development study in rats, the estimated exposures (Cmax and AUC0-24hr) in pregnant females at the NOAEL were 134 µg/mL and 1990 µg·hr/mL, respectively, corresponding to 4.8- and 3.8-fold higher exposures than those observed with the clinical dosing regimen (375/125 mg). As described above, abnormalities in axial skeletal morphology, including vertebral, rib, and sternebral abnormalities as well as short tails, were observed in rabbits at doses associated with maternal toxicity and at a relatively low exposure margin to the NOAEL (2.4-fold). Maternal toxicity, including reduced food intake (up to 62% and 94% in the mid- and high-dose groups, respectively), reduced body weight (6-8%), and reduced body-weight gain, does not necessarily explain the occurrence of teratogenic effects in the offspring. Direct teratogenic effects of ensitrelvir in the absence of manifest maternal toxicity cannot be excluded in humans. It should be noted that, although ensitrelvir has no known endogenous primary target, it exhibited inhibitory effects on PDE4A1A, PDE4B1, PDE4C1, and PDE4D2 in vitro and inhibited LCAT activity in monkey plasma in vitro. Effects on cholesterol levels were observed in both non-clinical and clinical studies. The applicant provided an overview of known skeletal abnormalities associated with PDE4 inhibition or knockout. Given the different nature of those abnormalities compared with the skeletal findings observed in embryo-foetal development studies with ensitrelvir, it was hypothesised that the underlying mechanism is different from PDE4D inhibition. In addition, there is currently no clear evidence that LCAT has a direct and specific role in foetal skeletal development. Therefore, the CHMP concluded that the mechanism underlying the skeletal malformations observed in embryo-foetal development studies remains unknown. In pre- and postnatal development and maternal function studies in rats, administration of ensitrelvir fumaric acid during late pregnancy and lactation affected pup survival, growth, and development. Ensitrelvir fumaric acid crosses the placenta in rats and is excreted into the milk of lactating rats; therefore, a risk to breastfed infants cannot be excluded. Overall, the CHMP considered that the non-clinical findings on reproductive and developmental toxicity warrant restrictions on the use of ensitrelvir during pregnancy and breastfeeding, as well as in women of childbearing potential. In particular, a contraindication during pregnancy was considered justified, given the observed embryo-foetal toxicity and skeletal malformations in rabbits, the low exposure margin, and the absence of a clearly established mechanism. Furthermore, women of childbearing potential should be advised to avoid becoming pregnant during administration of ensitrelvir and for 2 weeks after the last dose. For lactating women, breastfeeding is not recommended during treatment with ensitrelvir and for 2 weeks after the last dose. Wording of sections 4.3, 4.4 and of 4.6 of the SmPC was considered to appropriately reflect these precautions.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Local Tolerance

Since the intended therapeutic route is oral, no designated local tolerance study of ensitrelvir fumaric acid was conducted. Vomiting and decreased food consumption were commonly observed in the toxicity studies, however, no irritative or degenerative findings in the upper gastrointestinal tract were observed in any of the studies.

## Other studies

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Phototoxicity

The CHMP concluded that ensitrelvir fumaric acid is negative for phototoxicity in BALB/c 3T3 cells and therefore the risk of phototoxicity in humans is considered to be low.

## Mechanistic Studies for HDL- Cholesterol Reduction

The results of in vitro studies suggested that ensitrelvir inhibited LCAT activity in monkey plasma but not the cholesterol efflux activity by HDL.

Ensitrelvir fumaric acid was orally administered to cynomolgus monkeys repeatedly for 10 days (dose level: 10 mg/kg/day). From Day 2, total blood cholesterol decreased, accompanied by decreased HDL cholesterol and LDL cholesterol. No changes were observed in total cholesterol and its metabolite in faeces and bile, suggesting that the observed decrease in blood cholesterol was not caused by an effect on cholesterol excretion.

The applicant argued that there was little concern that the effects on lipids would lead to further adverse events in the clinic. However, the CHMP concluded that clinical relevance of effects on decreased cholesterol on the developing foetus cannot be excluded.

## Impurities

Identified mutagenic impurities were controlled in accordance with ICH M7.

## 4.5.2. Conclusions

The non-clinical pharmacology and pharmacokinetic profile of ensitrelvir have been well characterised. Based on the assessment of the available data, no specific non-clinical safety concerns have been identified.

The non-clinical toxicology profile of ensitrelvir has been adequately characterised. The most prominent findings were embryotoxicity and teratogenicity in rabbits, as well as effects on pup survival, growth, and development, for which a direct relationship to maternal toxicity could not be conclusively established and for which the margins to clinical exposure were low. The key non-clinical safety data, as well the appropriate precautions for pregnant and lactating women and women of childbearing potential are included in the SmPC.

## 5. Clinical aspects

## 5.1. Introduction

## 5.1.1. Good Clinical Practice (GCP) aspects

The clinical trials were performed in accordance with GCP as claimed by the applicant.

Based on the review of clinical data CHMP did not identify the need for a GCP inspection of the clinical trials included in this dossier (see section 2.4.3).

## 5.2. Clinical pharmacology

## 5.2.1. Methods

## Bioanalytical methods

Ensitrelvir in human samples was determined by liquid chromatography with tandem mass spectrometry (LC-MS/MS). Validated analytical methods were used for the quantitation of ensitrelvir in human plasma and urine, for the quantitation of the metabolite ensitrelvir indazole 4-Cl in plasma, and for the quantitation in plasma of drugs co-administered with ensitrelvir in drug-drug interaction studies. Determination of ensitrelvir in human samples was conducted within the established stability period and under the established stability conditions. Cross-validation of the analytical method for ensitrelvir in plasma samples was conducted between the different bioanalytical sites that performed the analysis, meeting the predefined agreement criteria.

## 5.2.2. Pharmacokinetics

## 5.2.2.1. Introduction

Ensitrelvir is a new chemical entity; the pharmacokinetic studies should therefore aim to describe its disposition and identify subgroups in which altered exposure can be expected based on its pharmacokinetic properties. Potential interactions should also be evaluated.

The study package contains a seven-part study including SAD, MAD, food-effect, and gender and ageeffect assessments; a mass balance study; studies in renal and hepatic impairment; and a number of DDI studies. It also includes an in vitro package characterising in vitro metabolism, transporters, protein binding, and the potential to inhibit or induce enzymes or transporters.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.2.2.2. Evaluation and qualification of models

## 5.2.2.2.1. Population pharmacokinetics

The popPK analysis S-217622-CPK-022-B as well as an updated analysis S-217622-CPK-024-B with an updated dataset is described below.

## popPK analysis S-217622-CPK-022-B

Pharmacokinetics were evaluated by population PK analysis (S-217622-CPK-022-B) using the plasma ensitrelvir concentrations obtained from participants at least 12 years of age Ensitrelvir 125 mg Tablets in Studies

- a 7-part Phase 1 study (Study 2102T1211) including SAD, MAD, food effect, DDI, effect of gender, PK in elderly etc.
- a Phase 1 hepatic impairment study (Study 2127T1213)
- a Phase 1 renal impairment study (Study 2128T1214)
- a Phase 1 DDI study with transporter substrates (Study 2130T1215)
- a Phase 1 DDI study with cytochrome P450 (CYP) 3A inhibitor or inducer (Study 2305T1218).
- 2108T1221 (Phase 2/3)
- ACTIV-2d/A5407 (Phase 3)

With 10449 samples from 2320 participants. A 2-compartment model with first-order absorption was selected as the population PK model. An exponential error model was used for the inter-individual variability, and a combined proportional and proportional residual error model was used for the intraindividual variability.

The final model was constructed after considering the effects of the following background data as covariates.

The body weight (BW), age, sex, race (Asian, White, or others), aspartate aminotransferase (AST), alanine aminotransferase (ALT), albumin (ALB), total bilirubin (BIL), creatinine clearance (CrCL), estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR), absolute estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFRabs), and health status (healthy, standard risk or non-hospitalized high risk infected by SARS-CoV-2) were tested as a covariate on apparent total clearance (CL/F). BW, age, sex, race, ALB, and health status were tested as a covariate on apparent volume of distribution in central compartments (Vc/F).

The effect of food [dosing within 2 hours after food intake (administration within 2 hours after a meal) or others (others)] and the effect of formulation (suspension or tablet) were tested as a covariate on first-order rate of absorption (Ka)

The effects of body weight on apparent total clearance (CL/F) and apparent volume of distribution in the central compartment (Vc/F), and the effects of food and formulation on absorption rate constant (Ka) were selected as covariates for the final model.

The population PK parameter estimates of the final model constructed are presented in Table 12.

The plasma concentrations at all sampling points in 175 participants were BLQ in SCORPIO-PEP study after S-217622 administration and the participants were excluded from the population PK analysis. ## 5.2.2.2.2. Physiology based pharmacokinetic model

Not applicable

## 5.2.2.3. Absorption

The absorption of ensitrelvir was studied in Phase 1 Study 2102T1211 (healthy adults) and consisted of seven parts. Single ascending doses of 20, 70, 250, 500, 1000, and 2000 mg and multiple doses of 750 mg / 375 mg and 375 mg /125 mg were investigated. After an orally administered single dose, Tmax was reached after ~ 2-3 h and the t1/2 ranged between 42-48 h. In general, the pharmacokinetics of ensitrelvir is linear.

Following administration of the clinical dose (loading dose of 375 mg on Day 1 and a maintenance dose of 125 mg on Days 2 to 5) to white healthy adult subjects, Cmax and AUCo-τ at day 5 (geometric mean (CV%)) were 24.8 (13.4) μg/mL and 475.5 (15.0) μg·hr/mL respectively (study 2102T1211).

Based on an in vitro-study, ensitrelvir is a substrate of both P-gp and BCRP. Passive permeability of ensitrelvir has not been determined.

## Bioavailability

A mass balance study was conducted but absolute bioavailability has not been determined. Data from the mass balance study indicate a relatively high absorption of an orally administered dose of ensitrelvir (see Metabolism below).

## BSC class

No claims for Biopharmaceutics Classification System (BSC) class have been made.

## 5.2.2.4. Bioequivalence

Ensitrelvir oral suspension was used in the single ascending-dose part of the first-in-human Phase 1 study (Study 2102T1211) and in the mass balance study (Study 2135T1216). In subsequent parts of Study 2102T1211, in the other Phase 1 studies, and in Phase 2 and 3 clinical studies, the 125- and/or 250-mg oral tablets (immediate release) of ensitrelvir were administered.

The 125- and 250-mg oral tablet formulations used in clinical studies contained standard excipients in the same w/w percentages; a common blend and standard dry granulation process were employed for production. For commercialisation, the profile of the 125-mg tablet was slightly changed from biconvex to flat-faced bevel-edged to minimise capping, while the round tablet shape was retained and the formulation remained unchanged.

## Influence of food

In Study 2102T1211, the effect of a high-calorie, high-fat meal on the PK of ensitrelvir was evaluated for the oral suspension (Cohort C) and oral tablets (Cohort P) in a two-group, two-period crossover study design.

When a single oral dose of 375 mg (3 × 125-mg tablets) was administered under fed (high-calorie, high-fat meal) conditions, the AUC0-last and AUC0-inf of ensitrelvir were increased by 24.4% and 24.5%, respectively, compared with fasted conditions, and Tmax was delayed from 2.50 to 6.00 hours. The Cmax values were comparable under fasted and fed conditions. In the Phase 2/3 study (Study 2108T1221)

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

and Phase 3 studies (Study ACTIV-2d/A5407 and Study 2206T1331), ensitrelvir was administered without food restrictions.

## 5.2.2.5. Distribution

Protein binding was 97.7% to 98.7% and was concentration-independent in the studied range (0.5-50 µg/mL). In the DDI cut-off calculations, the protein binding value at 50 µg/mL, i.e., 97.7% (fu 2.3%), was used. Ensitrelvir is predominantly bound to albumin.

The blood/plasma ratio was investigated in vitro, with results in the range of 0.56-0.57. Similar results were obtained in the mass balance study.

In healthy adult males who received a single oral dose of ensitrelvir (suspension) of 20 to 2000 mg in the fasted state, geometric mean Vz/F ranged from 13.5 to 20.6 L.

## 5.2.2.6. Metabolism

Figure 4. Elimination pathways from the mass balance studies

<!-- image -->

*Met 1: ensitrelvir triazole N-desmethyl, Met 2: ensitrelvir indazole N-desmethyl, Met 3: N-deindazolated form of ensitrelvir (presumed structure)

## Metabolism in vivo in humans

The absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion following single oral administration of 375 mg [ 14 C]-ensitrelvir (suspension, 92.2 μCi) in the fasted state were assessed in six healthy adult males. The median Tmax of ensitrelvir was 2.033 hours. The geometric mean plasma t1/2,z of ensitrelvir was 39.7 hours. The geometric mean ratio for the AUC0-inf of plasma ensitrelvir versus plasma total radioactivity (plasma ensitrelvir/plasma total radioactivity) was 0.896, suggesting that the main component in plasma after oral administration was unchanged ensitrelvir.

Of the radioactivity administered, 90.6% was recovered over a 456-hour sampling period. Of the total radioactivity administered, 64.8% was recovered in the faeces by the end of the sampling period (456 hours postdose). The radioactivity recovered in the faeces within 48 hours postdose accounted for 14.0%. This result, together with urinary recovery of 25.8% of total radioactivity, indicates favourable oral absorption of ensitrelvir.

The PK parameters of whole blood and plasma total radioactivity and plasma ensitrelvir following single oral administration of [ 14 C]-ensitrelvir are summarised in Table 16.

Following [ 14 C]-ensitrelvir administration, unchanged ensitrelvir was primarily detected, accounting for 95.57% of the total radioactivity in plasma. Ensitrelvir indazole 4-Cl was the only metabolite detected in plasma, accounting for 4.11% of plasma total radioactivity.

The results of urinary metabolite profiling indicated that 18.96% of the dose was detected as unchanged ensitrelvir and the sum of urinary metabolites accounted for 6.76% of the dose. The results of faecal metabolite profiling indicated that 50.73% of the dose was detected as unchanged ensitrelvir and the sum of all faecal metabolites accounted for 11.98% of the dose.

## In vitro

In the in vitro metabolism study, the majority of the radioactivity remained as the parent drug. The metabolites detected were an oxidized and glucuronidated metabolite, two demethylated metabolites and trace levels of a chlorinated metabolite. CYP3A4/5 and possibly other CYP enzymes are involved in the formation of the demethylated metabolites.

## 5.2.2.7. Elimination

Following an oral dose, 64.8 % (50.7 % parent and 12.0 % metabolites) was recovered in faeces and 25.8 % (18.7% parent and 6.8 % metabolites) was recovered in urine. Thus, approximately 70 % of the dose ensitrelvir was excreted as unchanged drug and approximately 19% as metabolites. Based on these results, it was concluded that ensitrelvir is excreted predominantly as unchanged drug in bile and also in urine.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In the SAD study (2102T1211), after an orally administered single dose of the suspension formulation, the t1/2 ranged between 42-48 h and CL/F ranged from 0.219-0.315 L/h. In the mass balance study (Study 2135T1216), the geometric mean plasma t1/2,z of ensitrelvir was 39.7 h and CL/F was 0.363 L/h.

Based on in vitro-data, ensitrelvir is not a substrate of OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT1, OCT2, OAT1, OAT3, MATE1, or MATE2-K.

## 5.2.2.8. Dose proportionality and time dependency

## Single doses of ensitrelvir

Single ascending doses (SAD) of ensitrelvir were investigated in Part 1 of Study 2102T1211. PK parameters of ensitrelvir in Japanese healthy adult male participants who received a single oral dose of ensitrelvir (suspension) of 20 to 2000 mg in the fasted state are shown in Table 17. The Cmax and AUC0-inf increased in an almost dose proportional manner across the dose range of 20 to 2000 mg.

## Multiple doses of ensitrelvir

The PK parameters of ensitrelvir in Japanese healthy adult females who received once-daily multiple oral doses of ensitrelvir (tablet) at 375/125 or 750/250 mg are presented in Table 18. The Cmax and AUC0-τ on Days 1 and 5 increased in an almost dose-proportional manner in Japanese healthy adult females.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Multiple doses of ensitrelvir were tested in several cohorts of study 2102T1211 and at two dose levels (375/125 or 750/250 mg). For two cohorts, the Cmax and AUC0-τ on Days 1 and 5 increased in an almost dose-proportional manner, see Table 18. However, in part 5 of study 2102T1211 the AUC0-τ and Cmax on Day 1 and Day 5 increased in a less than dose proportional manner.

## Time dependency

Time dependency was investigated in Study 2102T1211 using multiple doses at two dose levels. The geometric mean accumulation ratio (Day 5/Day 1) for Cmax and for the area under the plasma concentration-time curve over the dosing interval τ (AUC0-τ) ranged from 1.0327 to 1.4531 and from 1.2331 to 1.6093, respectively. The Cmax and AUC0-τ values were slightly elevated on Day 5 compared with Day 1 values in Japanese and White healthy adult males.

## Intra- and inter-individual variability

The inter-individual variability in pharmacokinetic parameters was in general low to moderate.

The population pharmacokinetic analysis revealed an intraindividual variability of 99.1% for Ka, 40.0% for CL/F, and 16.0% for Vc/F, with a residual error of 21.4%.

## 5.2.2.9. Pharmacokinetics in the target population

In the population PK analyses, the effect of health status (healthy or standard risk or nonhospitalized high risk infected by SARS-CoV-2) on the PK of ensitrelvir was not identified as an influencing factor.

It was therefore concluded that no ensitrelvir dose adjustment is necessary based on health status (healthy or standard risk or nonhospitalized high risk infected by SARS CoV-2).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.2.2.10. Special populations

## Renal impairment

A dedicated renal impairment study (study 2128T1214) was performed in subjects with mild, moderate or severe renal impairment compared to healthy controls. Subjects were classified based on eGFR values calculated based on the MDRD equation and converted to an absolute measure. No major differences were observed in fu of ensitrelvir in plasma between the different degrees of renal function. Cmax and AUC0-inf of ensitrelvir were 1.32- and 1.44-fold in participants with mild renal impairment, 1.33- and 1.49-fold higher in participants with moderate renal impairment, and 1.11- and 1.60-fold in participants with severe renal impairment compared to those in participants with normal renal function. The applicant concludes that no dose adjustment is needed when ensitrelvir is administered to patients with mild, moderate or severe renal impairment. Ensitrelvir has not been evaluated in patients with End Stage Renal Disease including those requiring haemodialysis.

## Hepatic impairment

A dedicated hepatic impairment study (study 2127T1213) was performed in subjects with mild and moderate hepatic impairment compared to healthy controls. Subjects were classified according to Child-Pugh score. No major differences were observed for the unbound fraction (fu) of ensitrelvir in plasma between the different degrees of hepatic function. There was a tendency towards decreased exposure to ensitrelvir with increased severity of hepatic impairment. The Cmax and AUC0-inf of ensitrelvir were 0.89- and 1.03-fold in participants with mild hepatic impairment and 0.74 and 0.87fold in participants with moderate hepatic impairment compared to those in participants with normal hepatic function. The Applicant concludes that no dose adjustment is needed in patients with mild and moderate hepatic impairment. Ensitrelvir has not been studied in subjects with severe hepatic impairment.

## Gender

In Study 2102T1211, the PK parameters and geometric mean ratios in Japanese healthy adult males and Japanese healthy adult females who received once-daily multiple oral doses of ensitrelvir (tablet) 750/250 mg were compared. The ratios of Cmax and AUC0-τ in females versus males were 1.22 and 1.17, respectively, on Day 1 and 1.16 and 1.10, respectively, on Day 5, indicating no significant difference in ensitrelvir exposure between males and females. Further, gender was not identified as an influencing factor in the population PK analysis.

## Ethnic factors

In Study 2102T1211, the PK of ensitrelvir was comparable between in Asian (Japanese) and White healthy adult participants who received once-daily multiple oral doses of ensitrelvir (tablet) 375/125 mg. In addition, race (Asian, White, Others) was not identified as an influencing factor based on the assessment of the effect of race on the PK of ensitrelvir in the population PK analyses.

## Body Weight

Body weight was identified as an influencing factor on the CL/F and Vc/F in the population PK analysis. Based on the population PK parameters obtained, C24, Cmax, and AUC0-τ (C24\\_Day 1, Cmax\\_Day 1, and AUC\\_Day 1, respectively) after the initial dose of ensitrelvir (375/125 or 750/250 mg) were estimated for individual participants in Study ACTIV-2d/A5407 and 2108T1221 by Bayesian estimation and are summarized by body weight category in Table 19.

Thus, although the exposure to ensitrelvir tends to decrease with increasing body weight, it was concluded that no ensitrelvir dose adjustment based on body weight is required.

## Paediatric population

Ensitrelvir is proposed to be indicated in adolescents aged 12 years and older, with the same dose as for adults. No data is available in children below 12 years of age.

The plasma concentrations in adolescents 12 to &lt; 18 years of age, whose age and body weight distribution are presented in Table 20, were comparable to those observed in adults 18 years of age and older in Studies 2108T1221 and 2206T1331. Age was not identified as an influencing factor following the assessment of the effect of age on the PK of ensitrelvir in the population PK analyses.

No ensitrelvir dose adjustment is proposed by the applicant for individuals ≥ 12 years of age.
