## 5.2.2.11. Pharmacokinetic interaction studies

## 5.2.2.11.1. Ensitrelvir as object (victim) of drug-drug interactions

Based on in vitro-data, ensitrelvir is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP but not of OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT1, OCT2, OAT1, OAT3, MATE1, or MATE2-K.

Based on in vitro data, CYP3A4/5 and possibly other CYP enzymes are involved in the metabolism of ensitrelvir.

In study 2305T1218, the effects of multiple doses of the strong CYP3A4 inhibitor itraconazole and the strong CYP3A4 inducer carbamazepine (given for a total of 14 days with 7 days at the highest dose) on the pharmacokinetics of ensitrelvir were investigated. The Cmax, AUC0-τ and C24 of ensitrelvir coadministered with itraconazole increased by approximately 1.05-, 1.10-, and 1.27-fold, respectively, on the initial dose of ensitrelvir and approximately 1.24-, 1.31-, and 1.40-fold, respectively, on the last dose of ensitrelvir, compared to those of ensitrelvir administered alone, suggesting that ensitrelvir can be co-administered with strong CYP3A4-inhibitors. The Cmax, AUC0-τ and C24 of ensitrelvir coadministered with carbamazepine at the scheduled dose decreased by approximately 8%, 21%, and 35%, respectively, on the initial dose of ensitrelvir and approximately 38%, 46%, and 52%, respectively, on the last dose of ensitrelvir, compared to those of ensitrelvir administered alone. This suggests that carbamazepine had a clinically meaningful effect on the PK of ensitrelvir. The applicant concluded that concomitant treatment with strong CYP3A4 inducers should be contraindicated and that concomitant use with moderate CYP3A4 inducers should be done with caution.

## 5.2.2.11.2. Ensitrelvir as precipitant (perpetrator) of drug-drug interactions

Table 21: Cut-offs for the evaluation of interaction potential

|                         |   50×Cmax(u) a (µM) |   10x Cmax(u) a (µM) |   10×Inlet Cmax(u) a (µM) |   0.1×dose/250 ml b (µM) |
|-------------------------|---------------------|----------------------|---------------------------|--------------------------|
| Parent drug ensitrelvir |                60.8 |                 12.2 |                      29.9 |                    282.0 |

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In vitro inhibition of all mandatory CYP enzymes by ensitrelvir has been investigated. There was a signal of in vivo relevant competitive inhibition of CYP2C8 by ensitrelvir, based on the basic model. The Applicant proposes that the risk of clinically relevant inhibition of CYP2C8 can be excluded using a mechanism-based mechanistic static PK model. There was also a signal of in vivo relevant timedependent (mechanism-based) inhibition of CYP3A4 by ensitrelvir. An in vivo midazolam study has been performed as discussed below. UGT inhibition was also studied in vitro. There is no indication of a clinically relevant UGT inhibition by ensitrelvir. In vitro induction of all mandatory CYP enzymes was studied, and in addition induction of CYP2C8, CYP2C9 and CYP2C19 was investigated. There are in vitro signals of clinically relevant induction of CYP2B6, CYP3A4, CYP2C8, CYP2C9 and CYP2C19 based on the basic method. The Applicant proposed to use a mechanism-based mechanistic-static model to exclude in vivo relevant induction of CYP2C8 and CYP2C9. For CYP2C19, the Applicant stated that induction is unlikely since the fold increases were less than for CYP2C8 and CYP2C9. Regarding induction of CYP2B6, the Applicant proposed to quantify the effects on a CYP2B6 substrate based on the mechanistic-static model. The use of the mechanisticstatic model was however not accepted. Regarding CYP3A4 induction, there is an in vivo study with midazolam as discussed below. Inhibition of all mandatory transporters by ensitrelvir has been investigated in vitro, and in addition inhibition of OCT1 and BSEP has been investigated. There are signals of in vivo relevant inhibition by ensitrelvir on P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT3 and OCT1 but not on the other transporters tested. An in vivo cocktail study has been performed for most of these transporters, see below, but not for OAT3. The effects of multiple dose administration of ensitrelvir on the pharmacokinetics of the sensitive CYP3A4 substrate midazolam was investigated twice in study 2102T1211, first with a higher dose than the intended clinical dose, and then with the intended clinical dose. In both studies, the midazolam AUC increased more than 5-fold, which means that ensitrelvir can be classified as a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor. With the clinical dose, midazolam Cmax and AUC0-inf of midazolam increased by 2.80 and 6.77fold, respectively. The Applicant proposes that ensitrelvir should be contraindicated with medicinal products that are highly dependent on CYP3A for clearance and for which elevated concentrations are associated with serious and/or life-threatening reactions. The effects of multiple dose administration of ensitrelvir on the pharmacokinetics of the CYP3A4 substrates and co-medicated drugs dexamethasone and prednisolone was also investigated in study 2102T1211, at a higher dose than the intended clinical dose. Cmax and AUC0-inf of dexamethasone coadministered with ensitrelvir on Day 5 increased by 1.47- and 3.47-fold, respectively, when compared with those following the single-dose administration of dexamethasone alone. Five and ten days after the last ensitrelvir dose, Cmax and AUC0-inf of dexamethasone were 1.24- and 2.38-fold and 1.17 and 1.58-fold, respectively, compared to those following single-dose administration of dexamethasone alone. The Applicant proposes that caution should be used if combining ensitrelvir and dexamethasone. For prednisolone, AUC0-inf increased by 1.25-fold on day 5 compared to administration of dexamethasone alone, while Cmax was not significantly affected. Five and ten days after the last ensitrelvir dose, there was no significant effect on prednisolone AUC or Cmax. The effects of multiple dose administration of ensitrelvir (clinical dose) on the pharmacokinetics of a combined oral contraceptive (COC) was investigated in study 2403T1219. The combination of ethinyl estradiol and drosperinone was chosen since ethinyl estradiol is used as estrogen in most COCs and

drosperinone is more sensitive to CYP3A modulation than other progestins. Cmax and AUC0-τ of ethinyl

Assessment report estradiol was not affected while Cmax and AUC0-τ of drospirenone increased by 1.5-fold and 1.8-fold respectively following co-administration with ensitrelvir for 5 days. The applicant concluded that this increase in exposure is not clinically relevant, considering also the short treatment duration.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The effects of a single dose of 500 mg ensitrelvir on the following transporter substrates were investigated in a cocktail study (study 2130T1215): digoxin (P-gp substrate), rosuvastatin (BCRP substrate and OATP 1B1/1B3 substrate) and metformin (MATE1 and OCT1 substrate). The AUC and Cmax of ensitrelvir following a 500 mg single dose were similar to the AUC and Cmax of the clinical dose at day 5 in study 2102T1211 (cohort N). Co-administration with ensitrelvir increased digoxin Cmax 2.17-fold and AUC0-inf 1.31-fold, indicating that ensitrelvir is an inhibitor of P-gp. Co-administration with ensitrelvir increased rosuvastatin Cmax 1.97-fold and AUC0-inf 1.65-fold, indicating that ensitrelvir is an inhibitor of BCRP and/or OATP1B1/1B3. In this study, the Applicant also measured coproporphyrin I which is mentioned in ICH M12 as a possible endogenous biomarker for evaluation of OATP1B1/3 inhibition potential. Coproporphyrin I was not affected by ensitrelvir and the applicant proposed that the effects on rosuvastatin are likely due to BCRP inhibition and not OATP inhibition. For metformin, the 90% CIs for Cmax and AUC0-inf were within 80.00-125.00.

## 5.2.3. Pharmacodynamics

## 5.2.3.1. Mechanism of action

Ensitrelvir is an inhibitor of the severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 (SARS-CoV-2) main protease (M pro , also known as 3CLpro or nsp5 protease), which is essential for the processing of the polyprotein encoded by the SARS-CoV-2 genes as well as for the replication of the virus.

## Inhibitory Effect on SARS-CoV-2 M pro Activity

The inhibitory activity of ensitrelvir fumaric acid on M pro activity was investigated using a protease inhibition assay. The IC50 value of ensitrelvir fumaric acid for SARS-CoV-2 M pro activity was 0.0132 µmol/L. Also, the inhibitory activity of ensitrelvir fumaric acid was investigated on M pro activity with prevalent amino acid mutations.

A total of 7,404,490 amino acids sequences from GISAID as of 10 February 2022 were analysed. The SARS-CoV-2 3CL protease amino acid substitutions L89F, K90R and P132H were identified as prevalent mutations (&gt;1% mutation frequency) in all analysed data. The substitutions G15S, T21I, T24I, K88R, K90R, P108S, P132H, A193V, H246Y and A255V were identified as prevalent mutations in each individual VOC, VOI and VUM designated by WHO as of 31 January 2022.

Ensitrelvir showed enzyme inhibitory activity equivalent to the wild-type against SARS-CoV-2 M pro having amino acid substitutions of G15S, T21I, T24I, K88R, L89F, K90R, P108S, P132H, A193V, H246Y, and A255V. IC50 values ranged from 0.0080 to 0.0150 µmol/L.

## 5.2.3.2. Primary and secondary pharmacology

## Antiviral activity of ensitrelvir

To evaluate the anti-SARS-CoV-2 effect of ensitrelvir, the inhibitory effect on cytopathic effects in VeroE6/TMPRSS2 cells was investigated.

The antiviral activity of ensitrelvir for an ancestral SARS-CoV-2 strain, clinical isolates having a mutation in the spike protein, and a mouse adapted strain was also evaluated. The EC50 values ranged from 0.20 to 0.99 µmol/L using VeroE6/TMPRSS2 cells.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

There are a few natural mutations in M pro , and most Omicron strains have a P132H mutation, which is not the active centre. Ensitrelvir exhibits antiviral activity against Omicron subvariants having P132H mutation.

In addition to the SARS-CoV-2 variants presented in the table below, the inhibitory effects of ensitrelvir were investigated in VeroE6/TMPRSS2 cells infected with KP.3.1.1 and XEC variants. The EC50 values were 0.387 µmol/L for KP.3.1.1 and 0.309 µmol/L for XEC.

The stability of ensitrelvir in viral assay culture medium over a duration of 4 days was confirmed with a remaining concentration of at least 94.8%.

The anti-SARS-CoV-2 activity of ensitrelvir fumaric acid was evaluated in human airway epithelial cells (hAEC). The SARS-CoV-2 delta variant (Pango lineage: B.1.617.2: hCoV-19/Japan/TY11-927-P1/2021 strain), and omicron variants (Pango lineage: B.1.1.529 hCoV-19/Japan/TY38-873/2021 strain, hCoV19/Japan/TY41-702/2022 strain, and hCoV-19/Japan/23-018/2022 strain) were used in an in vitro 3D human airway epithelium model.

The concentrations of ensitrelvir fumaric acid in hAEC, which reduced the viral amount in the supernatant to 1/10, 90% effective concentration (EC90), ranged from 0.0514 to 0.195 µmol/L. The mean % of cytotoxicity of ensitrelvir fumaric acid at 50 µmol/L was 1.75% in primary human nasal epithelial cells.

## Isolation of ensitrelvir resistant SARS-CoV-2 virus

To isolate drug resistant viruses, virus passage was conducted in the presence of ensitrelvir fumaric acid at various concentrations for VeroE6/TMPRSS2 cells infected with the following:

- ancestral strain (Pango lineage A: hCoV-19/Japan/TY/WK-521/2020 strain);
- alpha variant (Pango lineage B.1.1.7: hCoV-19/Japan/QHN001/2020 strain);
- beta variant (Pango lineage B.1.351: hCoV-19/Japan/TY8-612/2021 strain);
- gamma variant (Pango lineage P.1: hCoV-19/Japan/TY7-501/2021 strain) and
- delta variant (Pango lineage B.1.617.2: hCoV-19/Japan/TY11 927-P1/2021 strain).

Following sequencing analysis of the SARS-CoV-2 nsp5 region after 4 passages, amino acid substitutions were observed at positions located near the enzyme active centre of nsp5, which encodes M pro . The detected variants included the amino acid substitutions of D48G, M49L, P52S, or S144A.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Furthermore, M49(M/L)+S144A, the mixture of virus with S144A and with double amino acids substitution of M49L and S144A, was also detected (Table 25 below).

## Drug Susceptibility Testing of SARS-CoV-2 Mutants to Ensitrelvir Fumaric Acid in Cultured Cells

The susceptibility to ensitrelvir fumaric acid and remdesivir was investigated in VeroE6/TMPRSS2 cells for SARS-CoV-2 mutants, which were isolated in the presence of ensitrelvir fumaric acid and with amino acid substitution in the nsp5 region. Fold-change (FC) values against each reference strain were calculated from the ratio of EC50 values of each drug between the virus with the amino acid substitution in the nsp5 region, and the virus without amino acid substitution (Table 26 below). FC values of ensitrelvir fumaric acid against D48G, M49L, P52S, S144A, and M49(M/L)+S144A mutants were 3.7 to 6.5, 12 to 41, 5.5, 8.5 to 11, and 100, respectively. FC values of remdesivir against each mutant were 0.40 to 1.8, showing no cross-resistance.

## Replicative Capacity and Drug Susceptibility Testing of Reverse Genetics-derived SARS-CoV2 Mutants to Ensitrelvir Fumaric Acid

The drug susceptibility for anti-SARS-CoV-2 activity in reverse genetics-derived SARS-CoV-2 harbouring amino acid substitutions of D48G, M49L, P52S, S144A, and M49L+S144A in the nsp5 region, were tested for ensitrelvir fumaric acid, remdesivir, nirmatrelvir, and REGN-COV-2.

In comparison with the wild-type virus without mutation, FC values of ensitrelvir fumaric acid against the virus with amino acid substitutions of D48G, M49L, P52S, S144A, and M49L+S144A in the nsp5 region were 4.3, 17, 3.7, 9.2, and 100, respectively. FC values of remdesivir, nirmatrelvir, and REGN-COV2 against each mutant ranged from 0.52 to 1.0, 0.68 to 1.4, and 0.91 to 1.0, respectively, showing no cross-resistance (table below).

Replicative capacity in the mutant virus was comparable to the wild-type, indicating that the mutation in the nsp5 region of D48G, M49L, P52S, S144A and M49L+S144A had no apparent effects on replicative capacity.

## Ensitrelvir Drug Susceptibility Testing against SARS-CoV-2 Mutants detected in clinical trials.

Among the amino acid substitutions in nsp5 regions detected in the clinical trials, the following was evaluated for the antiviral activity of ensitrelvir.

- Phase 2a Part of Study 2108T1221: amino acid substitutions of L87F, T198I, A234S, and H246Y
- Phase 3 Part of Study 2108T1221: T25A, T25I, D48Y, M49I, M49L, S144A
- Study 2206T1331: T25A, T25I, T25N, M49I, M49L, P52S

The EC50 values of ensitrelvir fumaric acid for T25A, M49I, T25N, and E166A were 14.4-, 5.25-, 4.84-, and 3.39-fold, respectively, compared to the wild type, whereas the EC50 values of ensitrelvir fumaric acid to other amino acid mutations evaluated were within 3-fold compared to the wild type. It was indicated that the susceptibility of ensitrelvir fumaric acid was reduced in T25A, M49I, T25N, and E166A.

## Cross-resistance

The amino acid substitution E166V is a mutation responsible for reduced susceptibility to nirmatrelvir. The antiviral activity of ensitrelvir fumaric acid for double amino acids mutations including E166V were evaluated.

## 5.2.4. Pharmacokinetics/pharmacodynamics (PK/PD)

## Relationship between plasma concentration and effect and safety

## Concentration-QTc Analysis

Concentration-QTc analysis according to Garnett et al, 2018a; Garnett et al, 2018b principles was performed to investigate the effect of ensitrelvir on the QTc interval using plasma concentration and electrocardiogram data from Japanese healthy adults who received a single oral administration of ensitrelvir (suspension) 20 to 2000 mg in the fasted state (Study 2102T1211 CSR).

Exposure in Japanese healthy adult females who received once-daily multiple oral administrations of ensitrelvir (tablets) 375/125 mg was compared to the highest exposure in the highest exposure in the concentration-QTc analysis. The geometric mean Cmax in Japanese healthy adult females on Day 5 were 28.1 μg/mL Table 30), which was approximately 3.4-fold lower than 96.9 μg/mL, geometric mean Cmax after single administration at 2000 mg (Table 31).

A linear mixed effects model was established to determine the relationship between changes in Fridericia-corrected QTc interval (QTcF) from baseline (ΔQTcF) and plasma ensitrelvir concentration. Within the range of exposure after single oral administration of ensitrelvir (suspension) 20 to 2000 mg, the relationship between plasma ensitrelvir concentration and placebo- and baseline-corrected Fridericia's QTc interval (ΔΔQTcF) was close to 0 msec at any plasma concentration (Figure 6), and the upper limit of the two-sided 90% CI was below 10 msec for all dose levels (Table 32).

Based on these results, it was concluded that there was no QTc prolongation in the plasma concentration range of ensitrelvir tested (geometric mean Cmax from 1.70 to 96.9 μg/mL) after singledose administration of ensitrelvir (suspension) 20 to 2000 mg.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.2.5. Dose selection and therapeutic window

No specific discussion of the therapeutic window has been provided by the applicant.

Regarding increases in exposure, the applicant referred in the study report to study 2305T1218 to safety being confirmed at the double intended clinical dose (750/250 mg) (study 2108T1221, SCORPIO-SR).

Regarding decreases in exposure, the applicant concluded that the approximately 50% reduction in AUC observed with a strong CYP3A inducer is clinically meaningful and that concomitant use with potent CYP3A4 inducers should be contraindicated.

## 5.2.6. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical pharmacology

## 5.2.6.1. Discussion

The pharmacokinetic profile of ensitrelvir has been well characterised in 7 Phase 1 studies, 1 Phase 2/3 study and 2 Phase 3 studies with two different solid oral immediate-release (IR) formulations (125 and 250 mg tablet formulations). In addition, the first clinical PK studies were conducted with an oral suspension of ensitrelvir with up to 2000 mg administered once daily.

## Methods

The bioanalytical methods used are considered adequately validated. The pre-study validation reports submitted for bioanalysis of ensitrelvir in urine and plasma were in accordance with ICHM10. Withinstudy validation reports were submitted for all studies. Long-term stability of the analyte in matrix for a period covering the time from first sample collection until last sample analysis was demonstrated and ISR was performed for all studies with acceptable results. Thus, the performance of the analytical methods measuring ensitrelvir in plasma and urine was satisfactory.

Validated methods were also used for determination of ensitrelvir in plasma ultrafiltrate, for determination of the metabolite ensitrelvir indazole 4-Cl and for determination of interacting drugs in the DDI studies.

## Evaluation and qualification of models

## popPK analysis S-217622-CPK-022-B

The first popPK analysis did not include the full dataset. The provided VPCs are difficult to interpret. The model included food effect, formulation effect and body weight-based allometric scaling with estimated exponents. In this analysis the lowest body weight was 35 kg, however an additional 1005 subjects including an additional 67 adolescents were included in the updated analysis.

With regards to the covariate analysis, it is noted that food effect was tested on Ka, however it would have been appropriate to also consider food effect on the extent of absorption. The CYP3A4 DDI study was included in the dataset. The DDI studies showed increased exposure for inhibition and decreased exposure for inducers. It is noted that this DDI effect was however not included in the model. Since conclusions can be drawn from the dedicated DDI studies, this is not further pursued.

## popPK analysis S-217622-CPK-024-B

This analysis includes PK data from an additional 1005 subjects including an additional 67 adolescents. The previous model structure was used as well as the covariates included (food effect, formulation, body weight based allometric scaling on clearance and volume of distribution, estimated exponents). The provided VPCs focus on the PEP study.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

With regard to the high number of plasma concentrations below the limit of quantification in the SCORPIO-HR study, the applicant clarified that BLQ observations in these participants are considered to have resulted from non-compliance with study medication rather than insufficient exposure. That is why PK data of these patients were excluded from the popPK analysis.

The popPK model can be used to support that the exposure in subjects with low body weight is safe. Adolescents as a population are expected to have a lower body weight than adults.

The allometric exponents were estimated. When it comes to paediatric subjects, the Modeling and Simulation Q&amp;A recommends fixed exponents. This is particularly important if the model is used to simulate exposure outside of the studied body weight range. The applicant has confirmed that the lowest body weight included in the popPK dataset was 32 kg.

The applicant presented that according to Vital and Health Statistics, the fifth percentile of body weight for a 12-year-old boy and girl (the lowest age included in the analysis) is 37.3 and 36.6 kg, respectively. Based on the dataset including subjects down to 32 kg and based on that the slightly higher exposure in low body weight subjects can be considered safe, no body weight cut-off is needed in section 4.1 of the SmPC. This is generally agreed with, even if other sources such as WHO report 5 th percentile closer to 30 kg, the argument still holds.

The applicant has provided stratified VPCs as requested. The VPCs support that the model is adequate with regards to describing the exposure in adolescents as the popPK model can reasonably well describe the plasma ensitrelvir concentrations regardless of study, age and body weight.

The applicant has provided figures showing that for subjects with lower body weight, the exposure is higher for Cmax, AUC and C24, as expected. Thus, there is no risk of lack of efficacy with lower body weight. With regards to safety, the applicant has plotted the highest exposure from the 750/250 mg dose in the provided figures. The low body weight subjects are not close to this exposure for any of the metrics. This supports that the 375/125 mg dose is effective and safe for adults and adolescents, also with low body weight.

The applicant also argued that exposure in high body weight subjects is still above the non-clinical target plasma concentration (6.09 μg/mL). This argument was not accepted by the CHMP, as the proposed target, while useful for dose finding, cannot be considered proof that the dose is effective. High body weight subjects were however included in the clinical studies, and thus high body weight subjects can be considered covered by clinical data.

## ADME

In general, the ADME of ensitrelvir has been well characterised by the applicant.

## Bioavailability

In the mass balance study, no i.v. dose was administered and absolute bioavailability of ensitrelvir cannot be determined. From the 64.8 % radioactivity recovered in faeces it cannot be firmly concluded if all the dose recovered is absorbed material of if some of it is unabsorbed drug. However, 14 % was recovered within 48 hours post dose and thus, the remaining approx. 50% of the radioactivity recovered in faeces after 48h is likely absorbed. In addition, 25.8 % of the radioactivity was recovered in urine. To conclude, the absolute bioavailability is not established, but data from the mass balance study indicate a relatively high absorption of an orally administered dose of ensitrelvir. No claims on bioavailability have been made by the applicant in the SmPC, which is acceptable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Ensitrelvir is a low solubility compound and the applicant has not shown that ensitrelvir has high permeability or complete absorption. Thus, based on current information it is considered a BCS class IV substance. The BCS class has not been discussed by the applicant which is acceptable.

## Bioequivalence

In the development of ensitrelvir, an oral suspension was used in Phase I studies (SAD, MAD and mass balance) and an immediate release tablet (125 mg and 250 mg) was used in phase I-III studies.

## Effect of food

To investigate the effect of food, the tablet formulation was administered to healthy adults at 375 mg in the fasted and fed state (high-fat, high calorie breakfast). In the fed state, the AUC of ensitrelvir increased by approximately 25%, Tmax was delayed from 2.5 h to 6 h and Cmax was not affected. This indicated that there is no clinically meaningful effect of food on ensitrelvir and, in the pivotal phase III study, ensitrelvir was administered without food restrictions. According to the recommendations in the SmPC, ensitrelvir can be taken with or without food, this is acceptable.

## Elimination and metabolism

The mass balance study was performed with an oral dose of 375 mg (which is the suggested clinical starting dose) given as an oral suspension containing a micro tracer [ 14 C]-ensitrelvir in the fasted state assessed in 6 healthy adult males. A single dose is sufficient since there are no major dose- or timedependencies. The total recovery was 90.6 % of the dose which is above the 90 % limit recommended according to the guideline on interactions and thus sufficient.

In plasma, ensitrelvir accounted for approximately 90 % of circulating total radioactivity based on AUC0-inf. Ensitrelvir indazole 4-CL was the only metabolite detected in plasma which accounted for 4.1 % of total radioactivity. Thus, no major metabolite was identified and over 90 % of the total radioactivity in plasma has been identified.

Following an oral dose, 64.8 % was recovered in faeces and 25.8 % was recovered in urine. In faeces, 50.7 % and 12.0 % were excreted as unchanged drug and metabolites, respectively. Although some excreted unchanged drug may represent unabsorbed drug, the degree of absorption is relatively high as discussed above (see bioavailability). In urine, 18.7 % and 6.8 % were excreted as unchanged drug and metabolites, respectively. Thus, approximately 70 % of the dose of ensitrelvir was excreted as unchanged drug and approximately 19% as metabolites. There were no metabolites recovered in urine or faeces that occurred at levels of more than 4.1 % of the dose.

To conclude, the main pathway of elimination was via biliary excretion of the parent drug with contribution from renal elimination. Also, there are no major metabolites recovered for ensitrelvir in plasma, and no further investigations of the metabolites are deemed necessary.

## Dose proportionality

After single oral doses of 20 to 200 mg in healthy volunteers, ensitrelvir was rapidly absorbed with median Tmax ranging from 1.5 to 4 hours. The AUC and Cmax increased approximately proportionally with dose.

Dose proportionality after multiple doses was investigated in several cohorts of study 2102T1211 using two dose levels (375/125 mg and 750/250 mg). After multiple oral doses (Day 5) there was a dose proportional increase seen in several cohorts but with some deviations in study 2102T1211, part 5. However, when taking the data together, ensitrelvir seems to have dose proportional pharmacokinetics also for multiple doses at the tested dose levels.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Time dependency

In the phase I study when Cmax and AUC were compared between Day 1 (loading dose) and Day 5 (last day of treatment according to the proposed dosing regimen) the exposure was slightly increased. For AUC and Cmax the mean accumulation ratio (Day 5/Day 1) was in the range of 1.2 to 1.6 and 1.0 to 1.5, respectively. This is not expected to have a clinically relevant impact.

## Therapeutic window

Regarding increases in exposure, it is agreed that a 2-fold increase in exposure is expected to be safe based on data with the double intended clinical dose from study 2108T1221. This is of relevance regarding treatment of patients with renal impairment as well as concomitant treatment with CYP3A inhibitors, where increases in exposure have been observed.

Regarding decreases in exposure, there is not much data to support whether efficacy is maintained at a decreased exposure. The applicant concludes that the approximately 50% reduction in AUC observed with a strong CYP3A inducer is clinically meaningful and that concomitant use with strong CYP3A4 inducers should be contraindicated and that it cannot be excluded that concomitant use with moderate CYP3A inducers could have a clinically relevant effect. The applicant proposed that the lower limit of the therapeutic window could be considered as 0.8 of the exposure of the applied 375/125 mg dosing regimen.

## Special populations

## Renal impairment

The renal impairment study was a single dose study using a dose of 375 mg (ie the clinical loading dose). A single dose study is sufficient since there are no major dose- or time dependencies. Renal function groups were defined based on absolute eGFR (ml/min) in accordance with recommendations in the 'Guideline on the evaluation of the pharmacokinetics of medicinal products in patients with decreased renal function', and the study definition of mild/moderate/severe RI and normal renal function is in line with guideline recommendations. Since fu was similar between the different groups in the renal impairment study, it is sufficient to compare total exposure. Generally, the results of the renal impairment study were consistent with the results of the mass balance study which showed that ensitrelvir is primarily eliminated via the biliary route with some contribution from renal elimination. Hence, as expected, renal impairment had a weak effect on the PK of ensitrelvir, with up to 1.3-fold increase of Cmax and up to 1.6-fold increase in AUC0-inf. It is agreed that the increases in AUC observed in the dedicated RI study (up to 1.6-fold increase in subjects with severe RI) are not clinically relevant considering the therapeutic window of ensitrelvir. Thus, it is agreed that ensitrelvir can be used without dose adjustment in patients with mild, moderate and severe RI. Subjects with end-stage renal disease (on dialysis) were not included in the dedicated renal impairment study. The applicant proposed to add information in section 5.2 based on a study investigating effects of haemodialysis, but due to the limitations of the study (no control group with normal renal function and it was not possible to confirm that study subjects were representative of ESRD subjects regarding GFR), addition of this information in section 5.2 was not supported. However, based on the information from the RI study with a maximum 1.6-fold increase in exposure with severe RI and considering the limited contribution of renal excretion of the parent drug to the total elimination of ensitrelvir (19%), as well as the expectation that any effects of renal impairment on non-renal pathways would be larger in severe RI than in ESRD patients on dialysis, it is not expected that patients with ESRD would have more than a 2-fold increase in AUC compared to patients with normal renal function. Therefore, it is acceptable to state in section 4.2 of the SmPC that no dose adjustment is necessary in patients with renal impairment.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Hepatic impairment

The hepatic impairment study was a single dose study using a dose of 375 mg (i.e. the clinical loading dose). A single dose study is sufficient since there are no major dose- or time dependencies. Since fu was similar between the different groups in the hepatic impairment study, it is sufficient to compare total exposure. There was no increase in exposure observed in subjects with mild or moderate HI compared to subjects with normal hepatic function. Notably, contrary to what was expected based on the mass balance study, which demonstrated that ensitrelvir is primarily eliminated via the biliary route, exposure of ensitrelvir (AUC and Cmax) decreased slightly with deteriorating hepatic function in the hepatic impairment study. Subjects in the moderate hepatic impairment group in study 2127T1213 were however not affected in parameters generally expected to be related to drug elimination capacity according to the HI guideline, and thus it cannot be excluded that an increased exposure would be seen in subjects with moderate HI that are more affected in these parameters. However, it is not considered likely that a more than 2-fold increase in exposure would be expected in subjects with moderate HI. The applicant concluded that no dose adjustment is needed in patients with mild and moderate hepatic impairment, which was agreed by the CHMP. Severe hepatic impairment has not been studied, and use in this patient group is not recommended, which is adequately reflected in the SmPC.

## Gender

When using NCA, the exposure (Cmax and AUC) was slightly increased (~10-20%) for Japanese healthy female participants compared to Japanese healthy male participants. In addition, gender was not identified as an influencing factor based on assessment of the effect of sex on the PK of ensitrelvir in the population PK analysis. No ensitrelvir dose adjustment is suggested based on gender, which was agreed by the CHMP.

## Ethnic factors

The PK parameters of ensitrelvir in (Study 2102T1211) were comparable between Asian (Japanese) and White healthy adult participants after multiple oral doses of ensitrelvir (tablet) 375/125). Also, ethnicity was not identified as an influencing factor in the population PK analyses. No dose adjustment based on race was considered necessary by the CHMP.

## Elderly

The PK of Japanese elderly participants and Japanese healthy adults was comparable. No dose adjustments were considered necessary for elderly individuals over 65 years of age.

## Body Weight:

Based on the dataset including subjects down to 32 kg and the slightly higher exposure in low body weight subjects being considered safe, no body weight cut-off is considered necessary in section 4.1 of the SmPC.

## Paediatric population

Generally, the exposure in adolescents was comparable to and overlapping the adult exposure. Adolescents, especially in the younger age span, are expected to have a lower body weight than adults. The lowest body weight included in the popPK dataset was 32 kg. The slightly higher exposure in low body weight subjects can be considered safe, and was thus accepted by the CHMP.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Drug-drug interactions

## Victim aspects

Based on in vitro data, ensitrelvir is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP, and CYP3A4/5 and possibly other CYP enzymes could be involved in the metabolism of ensitrelvir. However, the contribution of CYP3A4 metabolism to the elimination of ensitrelvir was expected to be limited based on the results of the mass balance study. This was confirmed in the itraconazole study, where multiple-dose treatment with the strong CYP3A4 inhibitor itraconazole resulted in a 1.31-fold increase in ensitrelvir AUC. Considering the therapeutic window of ensitrelvir, it is agreed that this increase in exposure is not clinically relevant and that ensitrelvir can be co-administered with strong CYP3A4 inhibitors. Since itraconazole also inhibits P-gp and BCRP, and considering also the therapeutic window of ensitrelvir, this study can also be used to exclude relevant effects of P-gp/BCRP inhibitors on ensitrelvir exposure.

Treatment with the strong CYP3A inducer carbamazepine at a dose of 300 mg twice daily resulted in a 46% decrease in AUC and a 38% decrease in Cmax of ensitrelvir, which is considered a clinically meaningful effect. Only 3 participants completed the study with the 300 mg twice daily dose, but similar results were obtained with subjects receiving a lower dose included. The proposed contraindication with strong CYP3A4 inducers is considered reasonable. A relevant effect of a mild inducer is not expected, while it cannot be excluded that a moderate inducer could have a clinically relevant effect, taking into consideration that there is not much data to support that efficacy is maintained at a decreased exposure. This is adequately reflected in the SmPC.

Carbamazepine also induces other CYP enzymes as well as transporters, which may have contributed to the observed clinically relevant effect on ensitrelvir exposure.

## Perpetrator aspects

The substrates and positive control inhibitors/inducers used in the in vitro studies are generally acceptable. Based on the basic method there are in vitro signals of inhibition of CYP3A4 and CYP2C8, induction of CYP2B6, CYP3A4, CYP2C8, CYP2C9 and CYP2C19 and inhibition of P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT3 and OCT1.

The applicant proposed to use the mechanistic-static model to exclude some in vitro signals regarding CYP inhibition and induction, but this was not accepted. These in vitro signals are adequately reflected in the SmPC section 4.5.

Based on in vitro data, there is a signal of inhibition of OAT3 by ensitrelvir, which is adequately reflected in section 4.5 of the SmPC.

Ensitrelvir can be classified as a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor, based on the results of the in vivo study demonstrating that Cmax and AUC0-inf of midazolam increased by 2.80 and 6.77-fold, respectively, following multiple-dose administration of the clinical dose of ensitrelvir. Similar results were obtained using a higher dose. It is agreed that ensitrelvir should be contraindicated with medicinal products that are highly dependent on CYP3A for clearance and for which elevated concentrations are associated with serious and/or life-threatening reactions.

Based on in vitro data, ensitrelvir is both an inhibitor and an inducer of CYP3A4. Although the net effect on midazolam is inhibition, a concomitant induction effect cannot be excluded. Thus, induction of PXR cannot be excluded based on the midazolam study.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The effects of multiple dose administration of ensitrelvir on dexamethasone and prednisolone were investigated at a higher dose than the clinical dose; this is acceptable as the effect is not expected to be larger with the clinical dose, considering also that effects on midazolam were similar with the higher dose as with the clinical dose. Mean Cmax and AUC of dexamethasone were 1.47-fold and 3.47-fold higher on Day 5 of co-administration compared to corresponding values for dexamethasone alone. The effect diminished over time after the last dose of ensitrelvir. For prednisolone, AUCinf increased by 1.25-fold on Day 5 compared to administration of dexamethasone alone, while Cmax was not significantly affected. The CHMP considered that caution should be used if combining ensitrelvir and dexamethasone and that the effect on prednisolone is not clinically relevant. Ensitrelvir has reproductive toxicity and is a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor and also a CYP3A4 inducer based on in vitro data, although the net effect on midazolam is inhibition. It cannot currently be concluded if other PXR regulated enzymes are induced by ensitrelvir. It is therefore appreciated that the effects of multiple dose administration of ensitrelvir on the pharmacokinetics of a combined oral contraceptive were investigated. Ethinyl estradiol was not affected, and it is agreed that the increase in exposure observed for drospirenone (1.8-fold increase in AUC) is not clinically relevant. Thus, there is no sign of decreased efficacy of oral contraceptives due to ensitrelvir treatment. The cocktail study investigating the effect of ensitrelvir on transporter substrates (digoxin, rosuvastatin and metformin) was performed using a single dose of 500 mg ensitrelvir. This dose is considered representative of the clinical dose since AUC and Cmax were similar to those following the clinical dose at day 5. The chosen substances are listed as substrates of P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1/3 and MATE1 respectively. No OCT1 substrates are listed in ICHM12 and this is not a mandatory transporter to investigate. According to the ITC transporter white paper, although OCT1 (mainly expressed in the liver) may be involved in distribution of metformin to the liver (the primary site of action), is not a determinant of metformin plasma levels since metformin is almost exclusively eliminated by the kidney. Thus, although no effect was seen on metformin exposure, it is not possible to exclude inhibition of OCT1 based on this study since metformin is not a sensitive pharmacokinetic probe for OCT1 inhibition. However, it is not considered necessary to perform an additional in vivo study or to include a specific warning in the SmPC regarding OCT1 inhibition, considering that OCT1 is not a mandatory transporter to investigate. Based on the metformin study, ensitrelvir is not an inhibitor of MATE1 (there was however no in vitro signal of clinically relevant inhibition of MATE1). The effects of ensitrelvir on the exposure of the P-gp substrate digoxin (Cmax 2.17-fold and AUC0-inf 1.31-fold increase) could be an underestimation since digoxin is not the most sensitive substrate for determination of effects on intestinal inhibition of P-gp, and effects may thus be larger on e.g. dabigatran etexilate. It is agreed that caution is indicated in case of concomitant treatment with sensitive P-gp substrates with narrow therapeutic index. The effects of ensitrelvir on rosuvastatin exposure (Cmax increased 1.97-fold and AUC0-inf increased 1.65-fold) indicate that ensitrelvir is an inhibitor of BCRP and/or OATP1B1/1B3. Since coproporphyrin I (endogenous biomarker for OATP1B1/3 inhibition) was not affected by ensitrelvir, it is agreed that the effects on rosuvastatin are likely due to BCRP inhibition and not OATP inhibition. There is thus no need to include warnings regarding OATP substrates in the SmPC. In conclusion, it was shown in vivo that ensitrelvir is an inhibitor of P-gp and BCRP but not of OATP1B1/3. Concentration-QTc analysis The applicant has conducted conc-QTc modelling using plasma concentration and electrocardiogram data from Japanese healthy adults who received a single oral administration of ensitrelvir. The highest dose was 2000 mg and the Cmax was around 3.4-fold higher than in healthy Japanese females receiving the 375/125 mg regimen for 5 days, which is the proposed dosing in the SmPC. Comparing exposure

to healthy Japanese female volunteers is considered appropriate and conservative, as they may have higher exposure than males (males are expected to have higher body weight). A 3.4-fold higher exposure in the conc-QTc analysis was considered sufficient. Overall, the analysis was considered acceptable and supported that there is not a clinically meaningful QT-prolongation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Mechanism of action

Ensitrelvir is an inhibitor of the severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 (SARS-CoV-2) main protease (M pro , also known as 3CLpro or nsp5 protease), which is essential for the processing of the polyproteins encoded by the SARS-CoV-2 genes as well as replication of the virus.

The inhibitory activity of ensitrelvir fumaric acid on M pro activity has been determined and the IC50 value of ensitrelvir fumaric acid for SARS-CoV-2 M pro activity was 0.0132 µmol/L.

In M pro with prevalent mutations such as G15S, T21I, T24I, K88R, L89F, K90R, P108S, P132H, A193V, H246Y, and A255V (&gt;1% in sequences identified as of Jan-Feb 2022) ensitrelvir maintained antiviral activity with IC50 values ranging from 0.0080 to 0.0150 µmol/L.

## Antiviral activity

The anti-SARS-CoV-2 effect of ensitrelvir was tested in VeroE6/TMPRSS2 cells. Most Omicron strains have the P132H mutation, which is not the active centre. Ensitrelvir exhibited antiviral activity against Omicron subvariants having the P132H mutation as well as against clinical isolates having a mutation in the spike protein with EC50 values ranging from 0.20 to 0.99 µmol/L.

In human airway epithelial cells (hAEC) the ensitrelvir concentrations that reduced the viral amount in the supernatant to 1/10 (EC90) ranged from 0.0514 to 0.195 µmol/L.

The mean cytotoxicity of ensitrelvir fumaric acid at 50 µmol/L was 1.75% in primary human nasal epithelial cells; thus, ensitrelvir showed anti-SARS-CoV-2 activity at concentrations lower than those causing cytotoxicity in human airway epithelial cells.

Ensitrelvir has anti-SARS-CoV-2 activity against multiple clinical isolates with mutations in the spike protein and Omicron strains including those with the mutation P132H. The anti-SARS-CoV-2 activity of ensitrelvir was observed at concentrations substantially lower than the concentrations that cause cytotoxicity.

Isolation of ensitrelvir resistant SARS-CoV-2 virus

SARS-CoV-2 virus infected VeroE6/TMPRSS2 cells were cultured in the presence of ensitrelvir to obtain resistant strains. Following sequencing analysis of the nsp5 region the following amino acid substitutions were observed: D48G, M49L, P52S, or S144A. Furthermore, M49(M/L)+S144A, the mixture of virus with S144A and with a double amino acids substitution of M49L and S144A, was also detected.

The following fold change values of ensitrelvir were calculated in comparison to the wild-type virus; 3.7 to 6.5, 12 to 41, 5.5, 8.5 to 11, and 100 in D48G, M49L, P52S, S144A, and M49(M/L)+S144A mutants respectively. In recombinant SARS-CoV-2 carrying D48G, M49L, P52S, S144A, and M49L/S144A FC values were 4.3, 17, 3.7, 9.2, and 100, respectively. Thus, a single substitution may result in a relevant loss of antiviral activity. No resistance selection under ensitrelvir pressure was analysed for the currently circulating omicron variants. The CHMP recommended conducting annual resistance analyses reporting on the frequency of amino acid substitutions that have been identified in the studies to date, and those that have not been identified, but are within 5Å of the binding site, in SARS-CoV-2 sequences from Europe and globally to determine levels of circulating ensitrelvir resistance.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Viruses with amino acid substitutions of D48G, M49L, P52S, S144A, and M49L+S144A in the nsp5 region showed comparable proliferation ability to that of the wild-type virus in VeroE6/TMPRSS2 cells, and showed no cross-resistance to remdesivir or nirmatrelvir.

The M49L as well as M49(M/L)+S144A confer high resistance to ensitrelvir.

Of the SARS-CoV-2 mutants detected in clinical trials there were mutants with reduced susceptibility to ensitrelvir. The EC50 values for T25A, M49I, T25N, and E166A were 14.4-, 5.25-, 4.84-, and 3.39-fold, respectively, compared to the wild type. The effect of these treatment-emergent amino acid substitutions on the time to resolution of symptoms or the amount of viral RNA and virus titre is not known.

## Cross-resistance with nirmatrelvir

The amino acid substitution E166V is a mutation responsible for reduced susceptibility to nirmatrelvir. The antiviral activity of ensitrelvir fumaric acid for double amino acid mutations including E166V was evaluated.

In the experiments conducted by the applicant, nirmatrelvir showed reduced antiviral activity against T21I+166V and L50F+E166V with FC values of 36 and 43 respectively while ensitrelvir had antiviral activity against these mutants,T21I+166V (FC 2.76) and L50F+E166V (FC 2.81).

The triple amino acid substitution L50F+E166A+L167F had reduced susceptibility to ensitrelvir with FC 59.5 and to nirmatrelvir with FC 9.87.

In a separate study at, cross-resistance was also evaluated. Ensitrelvir showed antiviral activity against E166V alone (FC 1.04), T21I+E166V (FC 1.39) and L50F+E166V (FC 0.26).

Thus, there was no cross-resistance against ensitrelvir observed in SARS-CoV-2 mutants with amino acid substitutions including E166V, which have highly reduced susceptibility to nirmatrelvir.

## Dose justification

The regimen in the clinical studies is based on preclinical and nonclinical studies indicating that a plasma concentration exceeding 6.09 µg/mL is expected to decrease the intrapulmonary viral titer by 2 log10 (TCID50/mL). In a study of healthy adults, two doses were tested, 375/125 mg and 750/250 mg, and both resulted in plasma concentration levels exceeding 6.09 µg/mL. In the clinical Phase 2b study, the effects on viral load were similar between the 375/125 mg and 750/250 mg regimens; hence, the lower dose was selected for primary efficacy evaluation in the Phase 3 studies.

Taken together, the dosage for treatment of patients with COVID-19 has been justified. The same dosage was used in the post-exposure prophylaxis study, which was considered acceptable by the CHMP.

## 5.2.6.2. Conclusions

The clinical pharmacology of ensitrelvir has been adequately characterised. Ensitrelvir targets the M pro of SARS-CoV-2 and has demonstrated antiviral activity against strains with spike-mutations as well as omicron strains, at concentrations substantially lower than those causing cytotoxicity. There was no observed cross-resistance with nirmatrelvir or remdesivir. Furthermore, ensitrelvir at the dose of 375/125 mg is an active antiviral, impacting viral load compared to no treatment in participants with mild to moderate COVID-19.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The CHMP recommended conducting annual resistance analyses to determine levels of circulating ensitrelvir resistance.

## 5.3. Clinical efficacy

## 5.3.1. Dose justification

## Preclinical data in support of selected doses

In preclinical studies, the concentrations of ensitrelvir fumaric acid in human airway epithelial cells, which reduced the viral amount in the supernatant to 1/10, (90% effective concentration [EC90]), ranged from 0.0514 to 0.195 µmol/L. The estimated protein adjusted-EC50 value of ensitrelvir fumaric acid in the presence of 100% human serum and 100% mouse serum was 3.02 μmol/L and 3.93 μmol/L, respectively, which resulted in a 6-fold shift in potency of ensitrelvir in both mouse and human serum compared to the anti-viral activity in the absence of serum. Taking the potency shift in consideration, the necessary plasma ensitrelvir concentrations (C48hr) in mice to decrease intrapulmonary viral titer by 1 log10, 2 log10, and 3 log10 (TCID50/mL) were 1.61, 7.90, and 30.7 µg/mL, respectively, and the corresponding human plasma ensitrelvir concentrations were estimated to be 1.24, 6.09, and 23.7 µg/mL, considering the potency shifts with mouse and human plasma, respectively.

For the evaluation of a prophylactic effect, 24 hours after a single subcutaneous administration of ensitrelvir, mice were intranasally inoculated with SARS-CoV-2 and the prophylactic effect was evaluated based on survival time and body weight. A dose of ensitrelvir ≥ 64 mg/kg significantly reduced mortality and suppressed body weight loss. The plasma drug concentration 24 hours after administration of 64 mg/kg ensitrelvir was 2.99 µg/mL. By maintaining plasma ensitrelvir concentrations in humans at least at this same level, a prophylactic effect of ensitrelvir against SARSCoV-2 was expected to be demonstrated in humans.

## Justification for selected clinical doses

To reach the plasma ensitrelvir concentration needed for an antiviral effect of treatment soon after dosing and to maintain that plasma ensitrelvir concentration whilst minimizing cumulative exposure due to repeated administration, it was considered appropriate to combine a loading dose with daily maintenance doses.

Two different dose regimens were selected: 375 mg on Day 1 only as a loading dose, followed by 125 mg on Days 2 to 5 as a maintenance dose (375/125 mg), or 750 mg on Day 1 only as a loading dose, followed by 250 mg on Days 2 to 5 as a maintenance dose (750/250 mg) as the to-be-tested dosage in the following studies.

In white healthy adult participants following multiple-dose 5-day administration of ensitrelvir (tablet formulation) 375/125 mg or 750/250 mg the Cmax plasma concentrations in humans exceeds 6.09 µg/mL, which based on data in mice was the plasma ensitrelvir concentrations (C48hr) expected to decrease the intrapulmonary viral titer by 2 log10 (TCID50/mL), see table below.

There was no clear effect on COVID-19 symptoms with either dose.

## Post-exposure prophylaxis

For the post-exposure prophylaxis Study 2206T1331, the dosing regimen selected was the same as that used for treatment, namely ensitrelvir 375 mg orally administered once daily on Day 1 and 125 mg on Days 2 to 5 based on nonclinical studies and the effectiveness observed in the treatment of SARS-CoV-2 infection.

## 5.3.2. Main studies

## 5.3.2.1. SCORPIO-SR (2108T1221)

## 5.3.2.2. Study title

A Phase 2/3 Study of S-217622 in Participants Infected with SARS-CoV-2

## 5.3.2.3. Study design

This study was Phase 2/3, multicentre, randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled study that evaluated the efficacy and safety of ensitrelvir in adults and adolescents with SARS-CoV-2 infection who were at high or standard risk of progression to severe COVID-19.

This study consisted of four parts: Phase 2a Part (for participants with mild/moderate or asymptomatic severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 [SARS-CoV-2] infection), Phase 2b Part, Phase 3 Part (for participants with mild/moderate SARS-CoV-2 infection), and Phase 2b/3 Part (for participants with asymptomatic/mild symptoms only SARS-CoV-2 infection).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The study parts consisted of an intervention period (Days 1 to 5), the follow-up period (Days 6 to 28), and the exploratory period (Days 29 to 337).

Participants were considered to have completed the study if he/she had completed the follow-up period. Only participants who agreed to participate in the exploratory period were evaluated in the period.

Participants were randomly allocated at a ratio of 1:1:1 to one of the 3 treatment groups, i.e., 375/125 mg ensitrelvir group, 750/250 mg ensitrelvir group, and placebo group. Efficacy and safety of ensitrelvir were assessed for 21 and 28 days, respectively.

Figure 7: Study schema (Common to Phase 2a Part, Phase 2b Part, Phase 3 Part, and Phase 2b/3 Part)

<!-- image -->

Randomization/

5-day

administration

* Visits for Day 3 and Day 5 were optional (however, administration of study intervention and entry of participant diary had to be conducted). Visit for Day 9 was optional in countries except for Japan.
- ** Performed only for participants who agreed/assented to participate in the exploratory period.

## Patient population

Eligible participants were male and female ≥ 12 and &lt; 70 years of age (≥ 19 to &lt; 70 years in South Korea) with laboratory confirmed SARS-CoV-2 infection and mild/moderate clinical disease.

## Key inclusion criteria

- Participants who were diagnosed as SARS-CoV-2 positive by any of the following tests within 120 hours before randomisation:

- Nucleic acid detection test using nasopharyngeal swab, nasal swab, or saliva*

* Qualitative/quantitative RT-PCR test or isothermal nucleic acid amplification method (eg, loopmediated isothermal amplification [LAMP] method or transcription mediated amplification [TMA] method)

- Antigen test (quantitative) using nasopharyngeal swab, nasal swab, or saliva

- Antigen test (qualitative) using nasopharyngeal swab or nasal swab

- Participants with time from COVID-19 onset* to randomization of ≤ 120 hours.

*Time point when the participant noted at least 1 of 14 symptoms of COVID-19.

- Participants with at least 1 moderate (COVID-19 score of 2 [see definition below under efficacy assessment]) or severe symptom among the following 12 COVID-19 symptoms at randomisation (excluding symptoms present prior to COVID 19 onset).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Or participants with at least 1 moderate (COVID-19 score: 2) or severe preexisting symptom (symptoms present prior to COVID-19 onset) that was considered to have worsened at baseline:

- General symptoms: Low energy or tiredness, muscle or body aches, headache, chills or sweating, and feeling hot or feverish

- Respiratory symptoms: Stuffy or runny nose, sore throat, cough, and shortness of breath (difficulty breathing)

- Gastrointestinal symptoms: Nausea, vomiting, and diarrhea

In Korea, for the Phase 3 part of the study, the inclusion criterion was defined as participants who had at least 3 moderate or severe symptoms.

- Body weight had to be ≥ 40 kg (For minor participants only).

## Key exclusion criteria

- Participants with saturation of percutaneous oxygen of ≤ 93% during wakefulness (room air)
- Participants who needed oxygen administration
- Participants who needed a respirator
- Participants who were strongly suspected to have worsening of symptoms of SARS-CoV-2 infection within 48 hours after randomization, in the opinion of the investigator/subinvestigator
- Participants with suspected active systemic infections requiring treatment at the time of randomization (excluding SARS-CoV-2)
- Current or chronic history of moderate or severe liver disease or known hepatic or biliary abnormalities (with the exception of Gilbert's syndrome or asymptomatic gallstones)
- Current or chronic history of moderate or severe kidney disease

Study sites: The study was conducted at 94 sites in Japan (65 sites), South Korea (26 sites), Vietnam (2 sites), and Singapore (1 site) in Phase 3 Part. There was no participant enrolled from the site in Singapore.

## Randomisation

Participants were randomly assigned to S-217622 125 mg group, S-217622 250 mg group, or placebo group at a ratio of 1:1:1.

Randomisation of participants with mild/moderate SARS-CoV-2 infection was stratified by time from COVID-19 onset to randomization (&lt; 72 hours/≥ 72 hours) and SARS-CoV-2 vaccination history. Randomisation of participants with asymptomatic/mild symptoms only SARS-CoV-2 infection was stratified by SARSCoV-2 vaccination history.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Blinding

The trial was double-blind using a placebo that was indistinguishable in appearance, labelling, and packaging. All relevant personnel, including the sponsor, participants, and investigator/subinvestigator, were blinded. Some unblinded staff were assigned in the sponsor and the contract research organisation for statistical analyses.

Unblinding by request of the investigator was to occur only in the event of an emergency or adverse event for which it was necessary to know the study intervention to determine an appropriate course of therapy for the participant. If the investigator considered it necessary to reveal the allocation of a particular participant, the investigator could open the study intervention allocation table using the interactive response technology in accordance with the specified procedure. Prior to unblinding, and if the situation allowed it, the investigator had to try to contact the sponsor in order to get additional information about the study intervention. If the sponsor could not be contacted in advance, the sponsor had to be informed of the unblinding as soon as possible. However, the allocation to study intervention was revealed at that time. The interactive response technology was to keep a record of the reason for unblinding, participant identification code, date and time of unblinding, and who unblinded.

## Description of trial intervention

Eligible adult participants were randomized to one of the following groups:

## 375/125 mg ensitrelvir group

Five days of treatment once daily oral administration at the following doses. 375 mg (3 tablets) on Day 1 and 125 mg (1 tablet) on Days 2 to 5.

## 750/250 mg ensitrelvir group

Five days of treatment once daily oral administration at the following doses. 750 mg (3 tablets) on Day 1 and 250 mg (1 tablet) on Days 2 to 5.

## Placebo group

Five days of treatment once daily oral administration of placebo. 3 placebo tablets on Day 1 and 1 placebo tablet on Days 2 to 5.

## Concomitant and rescue therapies

## Prohibited Concomitant Therapy

Use of the following therapies was prohibited from the time of informed consent to the completion of examinations on Day 28 or at the time of discontinuation.

- Approved drugs for the treatment of SARS-CoV-2 infection; Unapproved drugs for the treatment of SARS-CoV-2 infection (eg, interferon, convalescent plasma, monoclonal antibodies, immunoglobulins, antirheumatic drugs, corticosteroids [oral, injection, inhaled], ivermectin, favipiravir); Antiviral, antibacterial, or antifungal drugs (excluding external use); Antipyretic analgesics other than acetaminophen; Antitussives and expectorants; Combination cold remedy; Other study interventions

Use of the following therapies was prohibited from the time of informed consent to the completion of examinations on Day 28.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- CYP3A substrates

Use of the following therapies was prohibited from the time of informed consent to 10 days after the last study intervention administration or at the time of discontinuation.

- Strong CYP3A inhibitors
- Strong CYP3A inducers
- Strong P-glycoprotein (P-gp) inhibitors
- Strong breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP) inhibitors
- The substrates of the following transporters (substrates susceptible to interaction and drugs with a narrow therapeutic range); P-gp, BCRP, Organic anion transporter polypeptide (OATP) 1B1, OATP 1B3, Organic cation transporter (OCT) 1, Organic anion transporter (OAT) 3, Multidrug and toxin extrusion (MATE) 1

## Efficacy Assessment

## Virologic Examination

The investigator or his/her designee took nasopharyngeal swab from participants. SARS-CoV-2 virus titer and amount of viral RNA were measured by RT-PCR.

## Participant Diary

The participant himself/herself evaluated his/her COVID-19 symptom scores (see details below) and measure SpO2 (using a pulse oximeter) and body temperature (axillary temperature) twice daily (morning and evening) from before the first dose of study intervention on Day 1 to Day 9 and once daily (evening) from Day 10 to Day 21 at the same time whenever possible.

The results were entered by the participant in a study diary. The first time on Day 1 was before the first study intervention. When acetaminophen was taken for the purpose of antipyretic/analgesic, COVID-19 symptom score was not evaluated, and body temperature was not measured until 4 hours after taking it.

Of the 14 COVID-19 symptoms listed below, participants assessed taste disorder and smell disorder themselves using a 3-point scale of 0 to 2 (0, The same as usual; 1, Less than usual; 2, No sense of smell/taste). The other 12 symptoms were assessed using a 4-point scale of 0 to 3 (0, None; 1, Mild; 2, Moderate; 3, Severe) according to the protocol-specified schedule.

- General symptoms: Low energy or tiredness, muscle or body aches, headache, chills or shivering, feeling hot or feverish, taste disorder, smell disorder

- Respiratory symptoms: Stuffy or runny nose, sore throat, cough, and shortness of breath (difficulty breathing)

- Gastrointestinal symptoms: Nausea, vomiting, and diarrhoea

The time to resolution of 5 COVID-19 symptoms (primary endpoint) was defined as the time from the start of the study intervention to resolution of all 5 symptoms of COVID-19 (stuffy or runny nose, sore throat, cough, feeling hot or feverish, and low energy or tiredness).

Resolution of symptoms was assessed according to the following rules:

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

For the pre-existing symptoms that were present prior to the onset of COVID-19 and considered by the participant to have worsened at baseline (pre-treatment examination), the severity had to be improved or remain the same.

- Severe at baseline: Moderate, Mild, or None
- Moderate at baseline: Mild, or None
- Mild at baseline; Mild, or None

Note: The participants were asked only for the pre-existing symptoms (in the past 30 days) and presence or absence of symptom exacerbation due to COVID-19 and evaluated the severity at baseline (pre-treatment examination). To avoid recall bias, the severity of the pre-existing symptoms prior to the onset of COVID-19 was not evaluated.

For the pre-existing symptoms that were present prior to the onset of COVID-19 and considered by the participant not to have worsened at baseline (pre-treatment examination), the severity had to be improved or remain the same.

- Severe at baseline: Severe, Moderate, Mild, or None
- Moderate at baseline: Moderate, Mild, or None
- Mild at baseline: Mild, or None

Symptoms other than the above (symptoms that have not occurred before the onset of COVID-19, and occurred at or after baseline [pre-treatment examination]), the severity had to become none.

## ➢ Severe, Moderate, Mild at baseline: None

Resolution of COVID-19 symptoms was defined as the time when all 5 COVID-19 symptoms (primary endpoint) disappeared, improved, or maintained as shown above after the first administration of study intervention. If the condition persisted for at least 24 hours, the participants were considered to achieve this endpoint.

## Eight-Point Ordinal Scale

The investigator/subinvestigator assessed the participant's condition on the 8-Point Ordinal Scale of 0 to 7 according to the protocol-specified schedule.

The scores on the 8-Point Ordinal Scale are 0) Asymptomatic; 1) Symptomatic, no limitation of activities; 2) Symptomatic, limitation of activities; 3) Hospitalized, no oxygen therapy; 4) Hospitalized, with oxygen therapy (&lt; 5 L/min); 5) Hospitalized, with oxygen therapy (≥ 5 L/min); 6) Hospitalized, with ventilation; and 7) Death.

## Safety Assessment

Safety was assessed through physical examinations, laboratory tests, vital signs, and ECGs. All AEs/serious AEs (SAEs) were collected from the date of signing of the informed consent form (ICF)/assent through the end of follow-up period. The severity and seriousness of an event was determined by the investigator according to the protocol-specified definitions. The relationship of an event to the study intervention was determined by the investigator.

## Pharmacokinetics Assessment

For the measurement of plasma ensitrelvir concentrations, blood samples were collected on Days 2 and 6.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.3.2.3.1. Objectives and estimands

## Primary objective

To compare the effect in improving clinical symptoms following 5-day administration of ensitrelvir with that of placebo in participants with SARS-CoV-2 infection, based on the time to resolution of 5 COVID19 symptoms. The superiority of ensitrelvir versus placebo was evaluated.

## Estimand for the primary objective

## Primary endpoint

Time to resolution of 5 COVID-19 symptoms (i.e., stuffy or runny nose, sore throat, cough, feeling hot or feverish, and low energy [tiredness]).

Participants were considered to have achieved this endpoint if resolution of the 5 COVID 19 symptoms persisted for at least 24 hours.

The estimand framework was not used.

## Statistical methods for estimation and sensitivity analysis

## Analysis sets

When the phase-3 part of the trial started (under protocol version 7.0), the protocol stated that efficacy analyses would be conducted in all randomised participants who had a confirmed positive RTPCR result (at Visit 1 or Visit 2) and had at least 3 moderate symptoms. This primary analysis set was changed 3 times during the trial:

- Protocol version 8.0: All randomised participants with SARS-CoV-2 viral titer detected at baseline. The detection of SARSCoV-2 viral titer was to be confirmed by viral titer assessment based on nasopharyngeal swab sample.
- Protocol version 9.0: All randomised participants who had a SARS-CoV-2 infection (confirmed by RT-PCR).
- Protocol version 10.0: All randomised participants who had a SARS-CoV-2 infection (confirmed by RT-PCR) and had &lt;72 hours from the onset of COVID-19 to randomization.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

| Analysis Set                                                                | Description                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| ITT population with < 72 hours from the onset of COVID-19 to randomization  | All participants randomly assigned to the study intervention with < 72 hours from the onset of COVID-19 to randomization and having SARS-CoV-2 infection, as confirmed by RT-PCR with a nasopharyngeal swab sample on Visit 1 (preintervention)                                                                                                                                        |
| ITT population                                                              | All participants randomly assigned to the study intervention and having SARS-CoV-2 infection, as confirmed by RT-PCR with a nasopharyngeal swab sample on Visit 1 (preintervention)                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| mITT population with < 72 hours from the onset of COVID-19 to randomization | All participants randomly assigned to the study intervention with < 72 hours from the onset of COVID-19 to randomization, having SARS- CoV-2 infection, as confirmed by RT-PCR with a nasopharyngeal swab sample on Visit 1 (preintervention), and having detectable infectious viral titer at randomization, as confirmed by viral titer assessment with a nasopharyngeal swab sample |
| mITT population                                                             | All participants randomly assigned to the study intervention, having SARS-CoV-2 infection, as confirmed by RT-PCR with a nasopharyngeal swab sample on Visit 1 (preintervention), and having detectable infectious viral titer at randomization, as confirmed by viral titer assessment with a nasopharyngeal swab sample                                                              |
| Safety Analysis Population                                                  | All participants randomly assigned to study intervention and who received at least one dose of study intervention. Participants were analyzed according to the initial intervention they actually received.                                                                                                                                                                            |
| PK Concentration Population                                                 | All participants who received at least one dose of study intervention with at least one evaluable plasma concentration value. This population was used for the drug concentration listing and graphical presentations.                                                                                                                                                                 |

ITT = intention-to-treat; mITT = modified intention-to-treat; RT-PCR = reverse transcription polymerase chain reaction

## Statistical analysis of the primary endpoint

Before protocol version 10.0, the primary efficacy analysis was a log-rank test stratified by time from COVID-19 onset to randomisation (&lt;72 hours, ≥72 hours) and SARS-CoV-2 vaccination history. After protocol version 10.0, the primary analysis was a Peto-Prentice stratified generalized Wilcoxon test stratified by SARS-CoV-2 vaccination history (not stratified by time from COVID-19 onset to randomisation).

The censoring rules remained unchanged during the trial. Participants whose COVID-19 symptoms had not been confirmed to have resolved were censored at the time of the last evaluation of COVID-19 symptoms or the time of the last evaluation of COVID-19 symptoms by the day before the first administration of the prohibited concomitant drugs listed below, whichever is earlier endpoint:

- Approved drugs for the treatment of SARS-CoV-2 infection
- Unapproved drugs for the treatment of SARS-CoV-2 infection (eg, interferon, convalescent plasma, monoclonal antibodies, immunoglobulins, antirheumatic drugs, corticosteroids [oral, injection, inhaled], ivermectin, favipiravir)

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In a supportive analysis, participants whose COVID-19 symptoms had not been confirmed to have resolved were censored at the time of the last evaluation of COVID-19 symptoms.

For the primary endpoint, additional analyses were pre-specified and remained unchanged in the protocol:

- The generalized Wilcoxon test stratified by time from COVID-19 onset to randomization (&lt;72 hours, ≥72 hours) and SARS-CoV-2 vaccination history.
- Kaplan-Meier curves and the difference in median time to improvement of COVID-19 symptoms and its 95% confidence interval (CI).
- A Cox proportional hazard model stratified by time from COVID-19 onset to randomization (&lt; 72 hours, ≥ 72 hours) and SARS-CoV-2 vaccination history.
- Restricted mean survival time (RMST) with a 21-day investigation period. A pairwise comparisons between each S-217622 treatment group and placebo was performed.

## Multiplicity/type-1-error control

When the trial started, the protocol stated that each of the doses of S-217622 would be compared to placebo at a significance level of 0.0125 (one sided). This is a Bonferroni split of the full significance level of 0.025 (one-sided). However, protocol version 10.0 stated that the lower dose (375/125 mg) would be tested at the full significance level of 0.025 (one sided), while the higher dose (750/250 mg) would be analysed descriptively only.

The endpoints were tested in a fixed sequence:

1. Primary endpoint (time to resolution of COVID-19 symptoms)
2. First key secondary endpoint (change from baseline on Day 4 in the amount of SARS-CoV-2 viral RNA)
3. Second key secondary endpoint (time to the first negative SARS-CoV-2 viral titer test). This endpoint was included not included in the fixed sequence until protocol version 9.0.

If all the tests in the sequence were statistically significant, the testing sequence would be repeated in the ITT population (as of protocol version 10.0).

## Interim analyses

When the phase-3 part of the trial started (under protocol version 7.0), an interim analysis was planned for the purpose of stopping for efficacy when follow-up had been completed in 50% of the target sample size. An alpha-spending function was to be used for the analysis of the primary analysis of the primary endpoint (O'Brien-Fleming boundary) and for the key secondary endpoint (Pocock boundary). An Independent Data Monitoring Committee (IDMC) was to be established for the purpose of this interim analysis. However, the plan to have an interim analysis was cancelled in protocol version 8.0.

A Data and Safety Monitoring Board (DSMB) was established for the purpose of evaluating safety.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Sample size

When the phase-3 part of the trial started (under protocol version 7.0), the target sample size depended on whether the interim analysis was going to be conducted and whether one or two doses would be included in phase 3 based on the results of the earlier phases of the trial:

- Two doses and one interim analysis and 2 doses: 420 per group. Assuming significance level 0.0125 (one sided), power 80%, log-rank test, and drop-out rate 10% (due to negative RTPCR result).
- Two doses and no interim analysis: 405 per group. Same assumptions as above.
- One dose and one interim analysis: 350 per group. Assuming significance level 0.025 (one sided), power 80%, log-rank test, and drop-out rate 10% (due to negative RT-PCR result).
- One dose and no interim analysis: 335 per group. Same assumptions as above.

The target sample size was increased in protocol version 8.0 (the interim analysis was also deleted):

- Two doses: 595 per group. Assuming significance level 0.0125 (one sided), power 80%, logrank test, and 20% drop-out rate (due to non-detection of SARS-CoV-2 viral titer seen in phase 2b).
- One dose: 495 per group. Assuming significance level 0.025 (one sided), power 80%, log-rank test, and 20% drop-out rate (due to non-detection of SARS-CoV-2 viral titer seen in phase 2b).

The target sample size was decreased again in protocol version 9.0:

Protocol version 9.0:

- Two doses: 530 per group. Assuming significance level 0.0125 (one sided), power 80%, logrank test, and 10% drop-out rate (due to negative RT-PCR result).
- One dose: 440 per group. Assuming significance level 0.025 (one sided), power 80%, log-rank test, and 10% drop-out rate (due to negative RT-PCR result).

## Key secondary objectives

- To compare the antiviral effect following 5-day administration of ensitrelvir with that of placebo in participants with SARS-CoV-2 infection, based on the change from baseline on Day 4 in the amount of SARS-CoV-2 viral RNA.
- To compare the antiviral effect of 5- day administration of ensitrelvir with that of placebo in participants with SARS-CoV-2 infection, based on time to the first negative SARS-CoV-2 viral titer.

The superiority of ensitrelvir-treatement versus placebo in antiviral effect was evaluated.

## Estimand for secondary objective

The estimand framework was not used.

## Estimands for the secondary objectives

Key Secondary endpoints

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Change from baseline in the amount of viral RNA on Day 4 and
- Time to the first negative SARS-CoV-2 infectious viral titer test in participants with mild/moderate SARS-CoV-2 infection with &lt; 72 hours from the onset of COVID-19 to randomization in the mITT population.

The estimand framework was not used.

## Statistical methods for estimation and sensitivity analysis

## Statistical analysis of the key secondary endpoints

Analysis of covariance was used to analyse the change from baseline in amount of SARS-CoV-2 viral RNA. The covariates were SARS-CoV-2 vaccination history and amount of SARS-CoV-2 viral RNA at baseline. Before protocol version 10.0, the model was also supposed to contain the covariate of time from COVID-19 onset to randomisation (&lt;72 hours, ≥72 hours).

A log-rank test, stratified by SARS-CoV-2 vaccination history, was used to analyse the time to first negative SARS-CoV-2 viral titre. Before protocol version 10.0, the test was also supposed to be stratified by time from COVID-19 onset to randomisation (&lt;72 hours, ≥72 hours).
